<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/equiti-acquires-lexi-ai-combine-ai-human-medical-interpretation" hreflang="en">Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Equiti is acquiring Lexi AI to build a hybrid language-access model that combines AI language support with human medical interpreters.

Equiti, which sells medical interpretation services to hospitals and health systems through its Martti business, provides 24/7 access to qualified medical interpreters across more than 250 languages and dialects. Lexi provides multilingual, AI-powered medical interpretation. The acquisition deal merges Lexi's immediate AI language support, covering over 35 languages and dialects, with Martti and its network of qualified medical interpreters to help healthcare organizations expand language access.

The acquisition was announced October 28. Financial details of the deal were not disclosed.

With this acquisition, Equiti is building on Martti’s work pioneering video remote interpretation in healthcare more than two decades ago, according to executives. The use of AI can expand where and when language services can be offered, particularly for everyday interactions that may not currently receive interpretation support, Equiti executives said.

Martti was built alongside healthcare organizations to make qualified interpreters available by video. Lexi, in turn, has been developed with healthcare customers to extend immediate language support into more workflows. The acquisition brings those capabilities together, with human-in-the-loop design, patient safety and patient experience at its center, Equiti executives said.

The company saw an opportunity to combine its services with Lexi's AI expertise rather than build a healthcare AI interpretation product internally, Maureen Huber, Equiti CEO, told Fierce Healthcare.

"Lexi brings specialized expertise in AI, engineering and healthcare product design, along with experience putting that technology into the hands of care teams. That combination takes time to build," Huber said. "For more than 20 years, our work through Martti has been about making qualified human interpretation accessible within healthcare. We understand the service, the clinical environment and what it takes to support healthcare organizations every day. Developing AI and machine learning technology calls for additional, specialized expertise. We wanted people who had already been doing that work with healthcare in mind."

She added, "We spent time evaluating the category and learning from customers. Lexi brought together the people, product and approach we were seeking. Combining their expertise with ours gives us a stronger foundation to expand language access responsibly."

Combining AI and human interpretation makes language support more accessible across the patient journey, helping organizations improve care delivery while making better use of their resources, according to Equiti executives.

Healthcare organizations are increasingly interested in AI interpretation capabilities and are looking for guidance, Huber noted.

"Customers were asking: 'Where could AI help? How do we evaluate it? What is appropriate for our patients?' Healthcare leaders see the same advances in consumer AI that everyone else sees. They also must think about what happens when those tools enter a care environment, sometimes before an organization has decided how they should be used. They want a partner who understands both the possibilities and the responsibilities," she said.

She added, "I would describe that as inquiry and evaluation. There is real curiosity about extending language support to more of the patient experience, particularly before and after a visit. Our job is to help customers identify those opportunities while keeping qualified human interpretation central to care delivery."

Equiti calls this approach Modality Matters, a connected model that allows healthcare organizations to match each encounter with the appropriate modality — immediate, healthcare-purpose-built AI support for approved use cases and qualified medical interpretation where clinical complexity, regulatory requirements, organizational policy or patient preference calls for a human interpreter. With this approach, organizations have the flexibility to shift between modalities as clinical context and communication needs change in real time.

The combined portfolio of Equiti and Lexi's capabilities will extend language access into everyday interactions that often go unsupported, Huber noted.

Equiti is focusing Lexi's AI-powered medical interpretation on routine patient engagement, including before and after care, such as appointment communications, scheduling and nonclinical follow-up logistics.

"We see a significant opportunity for Lexi in that communication before and after care: helping patients exchange messages, arrange appointments and stay connected in their preferred language. Language access must extend beyond the clinical encounter. Think about what happens after a patient leaves the clinic. They receive a message about a follow-up appointment, but they need to ask whether it is at the same location, how to reschedule, or whom to contact. Those may be simple questions for the organization, but the answers determine whether the patient can take the next step," she said.

Qualified human interpreters remain the foundation for care delivery, Huber noted. "In conversations such as those about a diagnosis, informed consent, treatment or palliative care, the ability to clarify meaning, recognize cultural context and respond with empathy is essential. A medically qualified interpreter helps people understand one another through conversations that can be complex and deeply personal," she said.

Equiti said it can work with customers to define appropriate uses within their policies and applicable requirements, preserving patient choice and access to a qualified human interpreter.

Language access is also a strategic performance driver for healthcare organizations, as effective communication influences patient understanding and experience, clinical outcomes, workforce efficiency and financial performance.

Equiti's customers include Wellstar Health System, Corewell Health, NYU Langone and Lee Health. Lexi works with a growing pipeline of federally qualified health centers and community providers including Lowell Community Health Center, which serves one of the most multilingual communities in Massachusetts.

As general-purpose AI gets better at translation, a healthcare-specific interpretation platform has distinct advantages, Huber asserted.

"Healthcare communication involves clinical meaning, cultural context, jargon and acronyms during situations where people may be frightened, vulnerable or unsure how to ask a question. Our job is to remove those communication barriers, and that requires understanding the care environment, fitting into clinical workflows and knowing when a qualified human interpreter needs to be involved," she said. "That is where Equiti’s experience matters. We bring the relationships, service infrastructure and human expertise developed through more than two decades of healthcare interpretation, alongside Lexi’s technology and team."

Huber said she envisions broader uses of Lexi's AI language support in healthcare workflows. "A patient’s need to communicate continues after the conversation with a clinician ends. Lexi already supports text-based messaging, including translation of text provided by a care team, and can even leave voicemail messages for patients. That creates opportunities to help patients access information and communicate between visits. Helping someone navigate follow-up care is a natural direction for that work, like understanding the next step, connecting with the right office or arranging an appointment. The value is in making it easier for patients to act on the care plan they have been given and ask for help along the way," she said.

"We built Lexi to fit the way healthcare teams actually work, bringing immediate language support into the devices and workflows they already use,” Siddharth UR, co-founder and chief product officer of Lexi, said in a statement. “As part of Equiti, we can extend that experience across the patient journey and connect it to a broader language access model built around the needs of each encounter.”

Lexi will operate alongside Martti under Equiti, with the teams working together on implementation and integration across healthcare workflows. Equiti will work with each customer to determine how the solutions fit within its language access program, clinical environment, and organizational policies.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/omada-health-hikes-2026-outlook-after-strongest-quarter-ever-q3" hreflang="en">Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Omada Health saw its "strongest quarter ever" in the third quarter of 2026, CEO and co-founder Sean Duffy told investors and analysts during a Thursday evening earnings call.

"We delivered our highest quarterly revenue, gross margin progression, and our most profitable quarter to date," Duffy said.

The virtual chronic care provider, which went public last June, brought in $91 million in revenue—up 34% year-over-year—and reported gross margin of 76% on a GAAP basis and 78% on a non-GAAP basis, according to Duffy. Omada reported net income of $7 million in the third quarter, compared with a net loss of $3 million in Q3 2025.

The company also reported adjusted EBITDA of $12 million, compared to $2 million the year prior.

It beat Wall Street analysts’ estimates for revenue, though slightly trailed behind projections for total membership.

Total members reached 1.1 million, up 36% year-over-year, with trailing 12-month revenue per total member increasing 5% year-over-year to $294, according to financial results published Thursday. Average member tenure also grew 10% compared to the year prior, to approximately two years and four months. Member retention has improved to 58%, up from 43% in Q4 of 2024, executives said.

Omada Health President Wei-Li Shao said Q3 membership growth reflects "continued demand" for its cardiometabolic platform, adding that the company's diabetes and hypertension programs continued to lead growth.

The company's diabetes and hypertension programs, longer member retention and lower care delivery costs are increasingly driving both growth and profitability, executives said.

"Members stayed with us longer, and our ability to provide personalized care at scale reached a new level of efficiency," Shao said.

The company supports diabetes, hypertension, obesity and musculoskeletal conditions—launching a GLP-1 care companion solution last November.

Shao noted that Omada deepened its partnership with the Health Care Services Corporation (HCSC) last quarter to extend its prevention and hypertension programs into three additional states, which is expected to "potentially reach an additional estimated 1.5 million covered lives at launch in 2027."

"On the PBM side, we have commercial relationships with the three largest PBMs in the country, which together processed an estimated 80% of prescription claims in 2025," Shao said. "Our prescribing program is in the market now at Optum. These relationships give us the broadest distribution in Omada’s history for our programs."

Shao, who will take over as CEO Jan. 1, also noted that artificial intelligence solutions enable care teams to have more capacity to "apply their clinical expertise" while tailoring care to each member’s needs.

"This quarter shows what we have built at Omada," Shao said. "Our commercial relationships give us broad reach, and our cardiometabolic programs have driven growth. Our program mix has supported longer member tenure, and our cost to serve has decreased."

Chief Financial Officer Steve Cook told investors the company expects new enrollments to "moderate sequentially" in the fourth quarter, which he said is "consistent with historic seasonality."

Shao also noted that a number of employers have been spending more time making GLP-1 coverage and obesity care decisions, "given the size of the commitment, the range of solutions and the importance of getting these decisions right," which has extended the selling season deeper into the fourth quarter.

Omada Health's stock dropped 19% Friday following the Q3 earnings call on Thursday as investors pulled back due to concerns about the longer selling season and expected Q4 member decline clouding the company's 2027 outlook.

The company is raising its full 2026 revenue and adjusted EBITDA outlook. Cook said revenue is expected to range from $344 million to $346 million, up from its previous range of $334 million to $340 million. Adjusted EBITDA is expected to be in the range of $32 million to $34 million, up from $21 million to $27 million.

"Our outlook anticipates Q4 revenue below our Q3 level, reflecting the lower new enrollment and engagement activity we have historically seen during the holidays," Cook said. "We expect revenue to come primarily from continued engagement among existing members."

"L’Inter pensa a sé, a riprendere la Lazio (domenica impegnata a Napoli) e a ripartire anche in campionato dopo la frenata nel derby. E per farlo c’è bisogno anche di vedere l’infermeria svuotarsi. Roma maintain contacts with Endrick’s entourage

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Roma is already looking ahead to the January transfer window, particularly to strengthen Gian Piero Gasperini’s attack.

Among the names the Giallorossi are following is Endrick, the Brazilian striker from Real Madrid.

Despite public and official statements, the 2006-born player continues to find little room in the Spanish club.

This situation is keeping Roma interested, as they are evaluating the opportunities offered by the winter transfer window.

According to journalist Matteo Moretto, D’Amico is continuing to maintain contact with Endrick’s team, without abandoning the pursuit of the Brazilian talent.

His name therefore remains among those being monitored by the Giallorossi ahead of the January transfer window.

Marcus Thuram, Florian Thauvin, Mathys Tel... Zidane prépare une surprise pour l'attaque de l'équipe de France

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Ce vendredi 6 novembre à 18h00, Zinédine Zidane dévoile sa deuxième liste officielle à la tête de l'équipe de France lors d'une conférence de presse organisée au siège de la FFF à Paris. Avec des forfaits notables et le retour attendu du capitaine Kylian Mbappé, le sélectionneur tricolore doit recomposer son secteur offensif.

Deux places se libèrent en effet sur le front de l'attaque tricolore avant ce rassemblement de novembre. Ousmane Dembélé, touché aux ischio-jambiers, et Esteban Lepaul, victime d'un important œdème à la cheville, sont contraints de déclarer forfait. Un contretemps pour le sélectionneur. Toutefois, Zinédine Zidane va pouvoir compter sur le retour de son capitaine Kylian Mbappé. L'attaquant devrait d'ailleurs retrouver une place dans le couloir gauche, où Zinédine Zidane envisage de le faire évoluer. Pour entourer le capitaine, l'ossature offensive de son premier rassemblement reste en place puisque Bradley Barcola, Désiré Doué et Michael Olise conservent la confiance du staff.

Pour combler les places vacantes dans l'axe, Zinédine Zidane pourrait bien miser sur des profils expérimentés. Absent lors de la première liste de septembre, Marcus Thuram est ainsi pressenti pour faire son retour sous le maillot bleu. L'attaquant de l'Inter Milan apporterait sa puissance et son expérience internationale. D'ailleurs, une autre piste d'expérience est évoquée ces dernières heures. Florian Thauvin postule également à une convocation grâce à ses performances en club avec le RC Lens, où il totalise 6 buts et 8 passes décisives en 12 matches. Si son âge de 33 ans pourrait freiner Zinédine Zidane, son apport chiffré reste notable.

Au-delà des cadres confirmés, le staff technique garde un œil très attentif sur la jeunesse pour dynamiser les ailes. Mathys Tel fait ainsi partie des pistes suivies par les Bleus. L'attaquant de Tottenham représente une solution pour apporter de la percussion et de la polyvalence sur les ailes. Un profil offensif séduisant. Dans le même temps, Éli Junior Kroupi suscite également l'intérêt de l'encadrement tricolore. Déjà pré-sélectionné, le joueur de Bournemouth est apprécié par le staff des Bleus, même si son retour de blessure et son manque de rythme pourraient retarder son arrivée.

Ces choix en attaque s'inscrivent dans le cadre des deux dernières journées de la phase de poules de la Ligue des nations de l'UEFA 2026-2027. Dans le groupe A1 de la Ligue A, la France est en tête avec 10 points devant l'Italie qui compte 7 points. L'équipe de France se déplacera pour affronter l'Italie le jeudi 12 novembre 2026 à 20h45 à Milan, à San Siro. Les Bleus recevront ensuite la Turquie le dimanche 15 novembre 2026 à 20h45 à Bordeaux, au Matmut Atlantique, deux rencontres qui seront retransmises en direct sur TF1.

Mancini trains separately ahead of Sassuolo, Kone in doubt

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Roma approaches their match against Sassuolo with a crowded infirmary and several lineup questions for Gian Piero Gasperini.

Tomorrow night’s match at the Stadio Olimpico, against the team coached by former Giallorossi player Alberto Aquilani, will be the Roman side’s ninth match in just 27 days.

Certain absences include Mile Svilar, Devyne Rensch, and Paulo Dybala, with the Argentine continuing his individual training program in the last session as well.

The medical staff’s main focus is on Manu Koné’s condition, as he will try to recover at least for the call-up against the Neroverdi.

As reported by Il Messaggero, the French midfielder underwent personalized training yesterday and will undergo a crucial test today to assess his chances of returning.

According to Leggo, the player would like to return immediately to Gasperini’s disposal and subsequently answer the call-up to the France national team. Roma, however, prefers to maintain a cautious attitude, considering the long period of inactivity and the repercussions that have affected the midfielder’s recovery.

Pierluigi Gollini’s availability also remains uncertain, having been sidelined by a back problem that prevented him from participating in the Champions League away match against Manchester United. At Trigoria, medical staff are resorting to infiltrations in an attempt to allow the goalkeeper to recover in time for the match at the Olimpico.

His condition will be monitored until the final hours, but Devis Vásquez remains ready to start. This would mark the Colombian’s return to Serie A since his last appearance, on May 25, 2025, when he wore the Empoli jersey.

There are no particular concerns regarding Gianluca Mancini, who did not participate in the resumption training session due to a simple workload management issue.

The defender remains available and will compete for a starting spot alongside Balerdi ahead of the match against Sassuolo. Gasperini will therefore have to resolve several doubts before finalizing the starting eleven, especially in midfield and goal.

Gasperini chooses not to risk Dybala vs Sassuolo

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Paulo Dybala will be unavailable to Gian Piero Gasperini for Roma-Sassuolo, scheduled for tomorrow at the Stadio Olimpico.

The Argentine striker also performed individualized training in his latest training session, but was unable to recover in time for the match against the Neroverdi.

As reported by La Gazzetta dello Sport, Dybala will now miss seven consecutive matches, as he is determined to avoid any risk before the international break.

The goal is to return to the pitch only after regaining top physical condition.

The November break will be an important opportunity to recover the injured players and return some key players to Gasperini.

Against Sassuolo, meanwhile, the coach is expected to rely on Soulé and Mora to support Malen, who will lead the Giallorossi’s attack.

« D’habitude, j’ai beaucoup à dire pour défendre notre équipe, mais pas ce soir » : Ruben Amorim accable ses joueurs après le fiasco de l'AC Milan

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Quatre jours après le succès dans le derby milanais, l'AC Milan a sombré à domicile face à Ferencváros en Ligue Europa. Excédé par le visage affiché par son équipe, l'entraîneur portugais Ruben Amorim a recadré ses joueurs en conférence de presse d'après-match.

Le jeudi 5 novembre 2026, San Siro a été le théâtre d'une immense désillusion pour les Rossoneri, battus (0-2) par la formation hongroise lors de la 4e journée de la phase de ligue. Une prestation indigne des ambitions lombardes qui place l'AC Milan dans une situation délicate sur la scène européenne.

Face aux journalistes à l'issue du coup de sifflet final, le technicien portugais n'a pas mâché ses mots au moment d'analyser la copie rendue par ses hommes. Ruben Amorim a notamment qualifié la première période d'« affreuse », refusant catégoriquement de chercher la moindre circonstance atténuante à ce défaite devant le public milanais. Le ton est donné.

Pourtant galvanisé par sa prestigieuse victoire face à l'Inter (3-1) en Serie A le week-end précédent, le club lombard a déçu. Ruben Amorim avait procédé à une large rotation dans son onze de départ, où seuls Ramos, Rabiot, Musah, De Winter et Diawara avaient débuté le choc face aux Nerazzurri. Les Milanais ont craqué au retour des vestiaires sur une grave erreur de contrôle du gardien de 21 ans Lorenzo Torriani, aligné pour suppléer Mike Maignan suspendu, sur une passe en retrait du défenseur Koni De Winter sous la pression de Kristoffer Zachariassen. L'attaquant haïtien Lenny Joseph en a profité pour ouvrir le score à la 52e minute, avant de signer un doublé à la 66e minute.

L'expulsion directe de Yusuf Bamidele à la 77e minute pour un coup de coude sur Mario Gila a laissé Ferencváros en infériorité numérique pour le dernier quart d'heure, sans que Milan ne parvienne à réduire l'écart. Les entrées en jeu en cours de rencontre de Diego Moreira, Christian Pulisic et Luka Modric n'ont pas permis aux Milanais d'inverser le cours du match. En zone mixte après la rencontre, Koni De Winter a d'ailleurs partagé le constat général : « Ce sont encore ces moments de perte de concentration. Aujourd’hui (hier), rien n’allait du tout. Je ne peux pas l’expliquer. Tout est une question de mental : la façon dont nous abordons les matchs, la façon dont nous jouons. C’est notre point faible ».

Au-delà de la défaite, la situation comptable devient particulièrement alarmante pour les Milanais, qui pointent désormais à une inquiétante 28e place au classement général de la Ligue Europa avec seulement 3 points en quatre rencontres, soit une victoire et trois défaites. Pour tenter de redresser la barre, Ruben Amorim a insisté sur le manque de culture de travail et a érigé Strahinja Pavlović en modèle d'engagement, estimant que tout le monde devrait s'entraîner comme lui.

L'AC Milan tentera de réagir en Serie A lors de son prochain déplacement sur la pelouse du Genoa, programmé le dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 15h00 au stade Luigi Ferraris.

Inter, Chivu si aggrappa ancora a Thuram: sempre in gol contro il Como in campionato. La Francia...

Cristian Chivu non rinuncia a Marcus Thuram. Il francese guiderà l'attacco dell'Inter anche domenica, in occasione del match di campionato contro il Como. Un ultimo sforzo prima della sosta per le Nazionali per il numero 9 nerazzurro, diventato ancor più imprescindibile dopo l'infortunio di Lautaro Martinez. Così scrive il Corriere dello Sport: "Di riposare al momento non se ne parla. La sfida contro il Como di domenica, ultima fatica prima della sosta, potrebbe essere la nona gara consecutiva da titolare con l'Inter per Marcus Thuram, che da una pausa all'altra per le nazionali praticamente non è mai partito dalla panchina".

"Il momento del riposo potrebbe non essere nemmeno troppo vicino, visto che oggi pomeriggio il ct della Francia, Zizou Zidane, diramerà le convocazioni e l'attaccante interista potrebbe essere in lista (nulla però è scontato) dopo la mancata chiamata di settembre. L'eventuale convocazione potrebbe curiosamente metterlo di fronte ai compagni dell'Inter e farlo tornare a San Siro, essendo in programma Italia-Francia il prossimo 12 novembre".

"Prima di guardare troppo oltre però l'attenzione è puntata sulla sfida contro il Como, una squadra da sempre nel mirino di Thuram. L'ex Gladbach non aveva segnato nella doppia semifinale di Coppa Italia dell'anno scorso, ma in campionato ha sempre fatto gol quando ha giocato contro i lariani. In tutto sono 4 in 5 incroci, inclusa la doppietta in riva al lago della passata stagione nel pirotecnico 4-3 per la squadra di Chivu".

Set up model-radar: catch free LLMs that really make tool calls

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

model-radar is two small commands I run for my AI agents:

Neither asks a model for an opinion. They measure and act. Setup is about ten minutes, and the guide is run on a fresh Ubuntu machine every week.

Python 3.11 or newer, nothing to pip install.

Before adding a key, run both with no credentials:

That exit 1 matters. If the canary can't check anything, a timer should notice, not shrug.

One line per model: PASS, FAIL or BUSY. BUSY means rate-limited or timed out, and it's never treated as a verdict, it just gets tried next run. On 8 October that gave me 4 passes out of 8 probed, two 403s and two busy. One free model I'd tried earlier answered every request and never once called the tool.

Run it once without --dry-run to save a baseline. From then on it only probes models it hasn't seen.

Point it at whatever your agents use (a JSON file, or hermes-agent profiles), add a paid fallback ladder and a local model in config.toml, and run it every few hours from cron. Two hard failures in a row and it walks the ladder. Timeouts and 429s never count.

Anything with a key. The weekly run has none, so it proves install, config, help and the no-key behaviour. The probes and the canary's failover need your OpenRouter account.

Full guide: github.com/casareanderson/model-radar/blob/main/docs/SETUP.md

How do you pick models for your agents, and how do you find out when one quietly stops working?

Every Airline Flying From The US To Australia & The Routes Served In 2026

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Global Guy & Kevin Hackert | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Nonstop travel between the US and Australia remains one of the most important long-haul markets across the Pacific, connecting major US gateways with Australia's largest cities and several destinations beyond its traditional east coast hubs. In 2026, airlines are operating an extensive selection of services, with the US gateways of Los Angeles International Airport (LAX), San Francisco International Airport (SFO), and Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW) providing the majority of connections.

The market features a combination of established nonstop routes, competitive services, and newer connections designed to give travelers greater flexibility. There are four main airlines providing nonstop links between the two countries, with a total of 8,820 flights scheduled between them in 2026, according to the latest data from ch-aviation. This equates to a total of 2,610,162 seats, and more than 18 billion available seat miles across the various airline and route combinations. These totals illustrate the scale of the market, while individual routes reveal how airlines distribute capacity according to demand, network strategy, and the importance of their respective US gateways.

American Airlines serves Australia through a focused network linking Los Angeles International Airport and Dallas/Fort Worth International with Sydney Airport (SYD) and Brisbane Airport (BNE). The airline's scheduled services for 2026 include 365 flights between Los Angeles and Sydney, and 135 between Dallas/Fort Worth and Brisbane. The oneworld carrier also schedules 13 flights between Los Angeles and Brisbane, giving it a presence across several important transpacific city pairs rather than relying on a single gateway. Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) is the most prominent Australian destination in American Airlines' published network, with the Los Angeles service offering a major link between Southern California and Australia's largest international aviation market.

The Dallas/Fort Worth to Brisbane route provides another important option, particularly for travelers originating in the central and eastern US who can connect through American Airlines' major Texas hub. These services complement the airline's broader domestic network, allowing passengers from numerous US cities to travel to Australia without necessarily beginning their journeys on the West Coast. The airline's schedule also demonstrates the different scales of its individual routes, with Los Angeles to Sydney recording 110,960 seats and Dallas/Fort Worth to Brisbane offering 35,031. Its Los Angeles to Brisbane operation is substantially smaller, with 3,705 seats. American Airlines' network between the US and Australia in 2026 is summarized in the table below:

Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW)

Delta Air Lines operates an extensive selection of services connecting Los Angeles with Sydney, Brisbane, and Melbourne. The schedule shows 418 flights between Los Angeles and Sydney and 182 between Los Angeles and Melbourne. The SkyTeam carrier's 65 flights between Los Angeles and Brisbane add another option for passengers traveling between the US and Australia's east coast. Los Angeles to Sydney is Delta Air Lines' largest Australian route, accounting for 114,981 seats and 860,862,747 available seat miles. The Los Angeles to Melbourne service adds 50,050 seats, creating a substantial second connection to Australia. These routes give Delta Air Lines a significant presence in the transpacific market, while its Los Angeles connections allow passengers from other parts of the US to access its long-haul services.

Delta Air Lines' network emphasizes established destinations with strong demand for business and leisure travel. Sydney provides access to Australia's largest metropolitan area, while Melbourne attracts visitors interested in its cultural attractions, sporting events, and business districts. Although the airline's Brisbane operation is smaller than its Sydney and Melbourne services, it broadens the available choices for travelers heading to Queensland. Together, these routes demonstrate how Delta Air Lines combines its US network with demand across Australia's principal international gateways.

The airport handles millions of passengers annually.

Qantas has a particularly important role in the US to Australia market as Australia's flag carrier, with services connecting several American cities to Sydney, Melbourne, and Brisbane. The carrier's operation include 353 flights between Los Angeles and Sydney, 357 between Los Angeles and Melbourne, and 336 between Los Angeles and Brisbane. Qantas also operates nonstop services between San Francisco and Sydney, demonstrating that its US network extends beyond its principal Los Angeles gateway. The carrier's Los Angeles to Melbourne route offers 105,666 seats, while Los Angeles to Brisbane offers 78,740 seats. Qantas' Los Angeles to Sydney service provides 170,458 seats, making it one of the most substantial individual routes between the two countries. The airline's San Francisco to Sydney operation adds another important connection for passengers traveling from Northern California, reducing the need to travel through Los Angeles before beginning a transpacific journey. The oneworld carrier's route network is outlined in the table below:

San Francisco International Airport (SFO)

Qantas' route structure reflects the importance of connecting Australia's major population centers with the US, particularly for travelers visiting family, pursuing business opportunities, or taking extended holidays. Sydney and Melbourne serve as major international gateways, while Brisbane provides access to Queensland and its coastal tourism destinations. With multiple US departure points and several Australian arrivals, Qantas offers a network that serves both point-to-point travelers and passengers connecting through larger domestic and international networks.

United Airlines has a broad network of transpacific services, with routes from San Francisco, Los Angeles, and Houston to destinations ranging from Sydney and Melbourne to Brisbane and Adelaide. Its schedule includes 443 flights between San Francisco and Sydney, 362 between Los Angeles and Sydney, and 363 between San Francisco and Melbourne.

San Francisco to Sydney is the largest United Airlines route to Australia, with a total of 115,855 seats and 859,296,535 available seat miles scheduled for 2026. The Los Angeles to Sydney route contributes another 93,034 seats, while San Francisco to Melbourne adds 93,291. United Airlines also serves Sydney from Houston, with 154 flights and 37,163 seats, providing a useful alternative for travelers originating in the southern US:

The Star Alliance carrier's extensive US domestic network helps support these long-haul services by connecting passengers from cities across the country to its international gateways. San Francisco offers convenient access for West Coast travelers, while Los Angeles provides another major departure point and Houston expands the network into Texas. The airline's San Francisco to Adelaide Airport (ADL) connection is particularly noteworthy because it extends the available Australian destination choices beyond the country's more established eastern gateways.

It is the largest unserved market between Australia & the US. Find out more here!

Los Angeles to Sydney stands out as one of the defining routes in the US to Australia market, with American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, Qantas, and United Airlines all represented. Their individual schedules show 365, 418, 353, and 362 flights, respectively, giving a combined total of 1,498 flights across these four carriers in 2026. Delta Air Lines records the highest individual flight count, although all four airlines contribute substantial capacity. The route's importance reflects the strong connections between Southern California and Australia's largest city, supported by tourism, business travel, education, and visiting friends and relatives.

Los Angeles is also a major connecting hub for passengers arriving from elsewhere in the US, while Sydney offers onward connections to destinations throughout Australia and the wider region. The combination makes the route attractive to both travelers beginning their journeys in either city and those connecting through the respective airline networks. Competition between four major carriers gives passengers a broader choice of schedules, loyalty programs, onboard products, and connecting itineraries.

A total of 110,960 seats are due to be operated by American Airlines throughout the year, with 114,981 by Delta Air Lines, 170,458 by Qantas, and 93,034 by United Airlines on their respective Los Angeles to Sydney services. These figures should be read alongside the flight counts, since aircraft capacity and scheduling patterns can influence the number of seats available. The four airlines operate a mix of Boeing 787-9, Airbus A350-900, and Airbus A380 aircraft on this route, and collectively, the services underline the route's central position in transpacific aviation.

United Airlines' San Francisco to Adelaide connection is a noteworthy addition to the range of routes linking the US with Australia, as Adelaide has traditionally received less direct transpacific service than Sydney or Melbourne. The carrier will operate a total of 44 flights and 11,308 seats on this route throughout 2026. Although considerably smaller than the established Los Angeles - Sydney operation, the service offers a direct link to South Australia rather than requiring passengers to arrive at another Australian city first. Adelaide provides access to a different side of Australia, with attractions including its central market, nearby wine regions, coastal scenery, and cultural festivals. The city is also an important gateway for visitors exploring South Australia, including the Barossa Valley and the Fleurieu Peninsula. For US travelers, a nonstop connection can also reduce the need for an additional domestic flight within Australia, although recent reports suggest the services are far from full, leading to a reduction in flights for the rest of 2026.

This route broadens United Airlines' Australian network by adding another destination beyond the country's largest eastern cities. Its relatively limited capacity suggests a smaller operation than the airline's established Sydney and Melbourne services, but the connection provides another option for travelers with business, family, or leisure interests in South Australia. The route's longer-term significance will depend on passenger demand, operating frequency, and how effectively United Airlines connects Adelaide with its wider US network.

Security Risks of AI-Generated Code: Research and a Review Checklist

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at shipfastdev.com

AI-generated code usually works, but it is often not secure. In Veracode's 2026 tests, models produced code that compiled almost 100% of the time, yet failed security checks on nearly 44% of tasks, a pass rate that has not improved since 2025. The fix is not to ban AI tools; it is to treat their output like code from a contractor you have never worked with: review it, scan it, and test it before it reaches production.

Below is a summary of the research, the failure patterns that come up again and again, and a review checklist you can hand to your developer or agency.

In its 2025 GenAI Code Security Report, Veracode gave 80 curated coding tasks to more than 100 large language models. When a secure and an insecure approach were both possible, models chose the insecure one 45% of the time. Java was the riskiest language, with a security failure rate above 70%; Python, C# and JavaScript failed between 38% and 45% of the time. Models failed to defend against cross-site scripting in 86% of cases and log injection in 88%. Larger models did not perform significantly better than smaller ones.

The 2026 edition found the average security pass rate "sits at 56 percent — virtually unchanged since last year's report." The best model on its summer 2026 leaderboard reached 68%, which still means failing nearly one security task in three. Python code passed 63% of the time, Java only 30%. Coding-specialised models (51%) did no better than general-purpose ones (52%), and model size made no clear difference, although reasoning models kept an edge (56% against 51%).

Google's 2025 DORA report found that AI adoption is linked to higher delivery throughput but still has a negative relationship with delivery stability. Its explanation applies directly to security: without strong automated testing and fast feedback loops, more change volume leads to more incidents.

Security usually depends on context the model does not have: which input comes from an untrusted user, who is allowed to see which record, which secrets exist in your environment. Cross-site scripting, which depends on knowing where a piece of data will be displayed, had one of the worst results in Veracode's 2025 tests (86% failure). The model writes code that does what you asked; whether it is safe depends on what you did not say.

You don't need to read code to use this list. Ask your developer to confirm each point for every feature that touches users, payments or data. Items 1 to 4 apply before any change is merged, 5 to 7 cover dependencies and secrets, and 8 to 12 cover testing and process.

These questions belong in any evaluation of a development partner. If your app was built mainly with an AI app builder, read where vibe-coded MVPs break as well: missing data-access rules are the most common problem there.

Not by default. Veracode's 2026 tests found an average security pass rate of 56%, unchanged from 2025, even though almost all generated code compiled. It needs the same review and testing as any other code, and arguably more.

In Veracode's tests, Java: a 30% security pass rate in 2026, against 63% for Python. Language matters less than review, though: every language failed a significant share of tasks.

It can help spot issues, but the same models chose insecure options in a large share of Veracode's tasks. Use AI review as one layer, alongside static analysis, dependency audits, tests and a human reviewer.

If it handles personal data, payments or logins, at least run through the checklist above before launch. A full external audit makes sense once you have real users or sensitive data at scale.

A Backdoor Sat in the Firmware Partition of 1.3 Million Set-Top Boxes

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at blog.neticslabs.com

Capture of the Google Cloud Threat Intelligence post “Shutting the Door on the XCPE Botnet”, taken 3 November 2026 from cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

On 2 November 2026 Google Threat Intelligence Group published Shutting the Door on the XCPE Botnet, with the disclosure that Google and industry partners had dismantled a backdoor enrolled on more than 1.3 million set-top boxes. The attribution is the part that changes how the story should be read: the researchers assess with high confidence that the malware was developed, deployed and operated for financial gain by Videostrong Technology, an Android original equipment and original design manufacturer.

The malicious software arrived preinstalled, across many device models and brands, built directly into firmware over multiple years. Google found dedicated infrastructure, evolving configurations and tools for managing affiliates, and assessed with high confidence that Videostrong intentionally developed, deployed and operated the backdoor and botnet for financial gain. Its response was to cut ties with Videostrong and ban the company from making devices with preloaded Google apps.

Google's own account of how the code got past certification is worth reading twice. Certified Android devices pass a suite of compatibility and security tests, and the vendor used obfuscation to exploit a gap in those tests, loading malicious code after the device's first boot. Review processes changed as a result. For anyone who buys devices on the strength of a certification mark, this is the uncomfortable lesson: certification covers the tested state, and the vendor retains control over code loaded after first boot.

Netics editorial visual: the scale Google reported for the enrolled device population.

Three stages are documented, and the first one explains why removal fails. A stub package, shipped as x.cpe in some builds and com.oemservice in others, sits in the system partition and runs at android.uid.system privilege. On boot it checks the oem/ directory, installs the corresponding x.cpe package into /data/app/ and reinstalls itself after any user uninstall, which makes the device's own settings screen an unreliable place to look for the truth.

Stage two offloads the work. The backdoor acts mainly as the command-and-control communicator and passes module execution to com.abc.nexus, a file that carries native anti-analysis utilities to defeat sandbox detection and configures the monetisation modules. Since August 2026 that loader has been phased out in favour of tailored droppers that install a single module, delivered through the same command channel. Stage three is the modules themselves, arriving as native ELF daemons, as SDKs compiled into the loader's bytecode, or as secondary APKs fetched remotely. Google says a significant majority of the staging files—modules, shell scripts and legitimate applications—were hosted as GitHub releases on operator-owned GitHub pages.

Google Threat Intelligence Group figure, 2 November 2026: the XCPE ecosystem, from the infected set-top box and its backdoor through staging infrastructure to command servers and modules. Source: cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

The same bootstrap returns geolocation and STUN parameters so the device can find its public address and traverse NAT, a file-upload URL for logs and diagnostic dumps, and switches for individual advertising modules. All samples analysed to that point used a single broker hostname, mqtt.szbboys[.]com. Command traffic that looks like ordinary telemetry and a broker reachable over the public internet describe a detection problem that lives in network logs, where most small organisations look last.

The economic design is a rented shop window. Videostrong sold backdoor access to criminal affiliates, and the loaded modules turn the box into a residential proxy exit node, an ad-fraud engine, or a host for further backdoors. The named SDKs are specific: Linkfog, AppSalt and NetNut among the proxy networks, CBOXTV and HiddenView among the ad-fraud engines, Moyu and Spirit among the backdoors that load more modules. AppSalt is the residential proxy SDK owned by Honeygain, which is owned by Oxylabs, a detail that shows how ordinary the corporate chains behind these modules look.

Greed shows in the stacking. A single device can run several modules with the same function, and the researchers report as many as three residential proxy SDKs installed on one device. A compromised box becomes a metered egress point for someone else's traffic, with the household or office IP address carrying the reputation damage.

Netics editorial visual: the three-stage chain from firmware stub to monetisation module, and the step that ended it.

The partner list behind the disruption reads like a cross-section of the industry: Japan's National Police Agency, Lumen, HUMAN Security, Plume, Synthient and TrendAI.

This story turns firmware provenance into an inventory problem. A new image can fix a kernel or container flaw on every host that rebuilds. This backdoor was compiled into the firmware partition of devices already deployed in living rooms; the vendor is barred from certification and the hardware remains in the field.

Google's disruption made three moves worth noting for any operator. It took legal action against the domains and servers used to control the botnet and to install modules. It took legal action against command domains used by more than a dozen affiliate malware families, which the researchers assess severs access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices, since those affiliates distribute through other channels too. And it changed how firmware integrity is verified on certified Android TV devices.

Devices keep reporting while the broker answers, so the distribution below shows where the installed base sits.

Google Threat Intelligence Group figure, 2 November 2026: the global distribution of devices reporting the x.cpe backdoor. Source: cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

The operator's version of that work starts with a list. Which Android boxes, signage players, conference-room dongles and kiosk displays are on the network, who bought them, which vendor built the firmware and whether that vendor still exists in a position to ship updates. Most small estates cannot answer the last question, and that gap is the exposure this campaign exploits.

Google cut off command infrastructure and tightened firmware verification. Google took legal action to take down the domains, affiliate command servers are down, a manufacturer is out of the certified programme, and Google says it tightened firmware verification. A box already installed retains its backdoor. The researchers are explicit that the illicit market is resilient, with operators pivoting to new loaders or rival SDKs when a component is disrupted.

For a small organisation, the practical controls are unglamorous and cheap. Segment devices that need outbound internet from the rest of the network, deny egress to hosts nobody recognises, and watch for long-lived sessions to message brokers, which almost no television box needs. Prefer hardware whose firmware updates you can verify, and treat a bargain Android box from an unknown ODM as an untrusted endpoint with a screen attached. Prebuilt images carry their own provenance and inventory requirements. The earlier analysis of the Copy Fail container flaw argued from the software side; a full security audit and hardening is where an estate turns that reasoning into a list of devices it can defend.

“Backdoor operators, ad-fraud rings, and residential proxy providers operate as an interconnected supply chain.” The sentence closes the report, and it also describes the buying decision in front of any organisation refreshing its screens this quarter. The supply chain runs through the firmware partition, and the receipt is the only document most buyers keep.

Netics editorial visual: the report's closing framing, quoted from Google Threat Intelligence Group.

Right, I confess. I have an annoying new hobby. While record collecting has been the epitome of millennial hipster cool for the past decade, it's become a little too pricey for my liking, which is what sparked my interest in its arguably less sexy cousin, CD collecting.

So what's so good about it? First of all, they're dirt cheap. Charity shops are rife with random CDs, and sifting through them often brings a welcome burst of nostalgia. Also, it's more compact – no need for a whole shelving setup; bring back the humble CD wallet that won't interfere with your interior design trends.

With a high-precision laser pickup, the JA-310R offers lossless CD decoding and detailed audio while the SPDIF optical out ensures a clean sound. Library by Collective Architecture breathes new life into Scottish high street

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Film and photography by Jim Stephenson

The conversion of a dilapidated high-street building into a public library in Paisley, Scotland, shortlisted for the RIAS Andrew Doolan Best Building in Scotland Award, features in this video by photographer Jim Stephenson.

The film forms part of a series being published by Dezeen in collaboration with the Royal Incorporation of Architects in Scotland (RIAS) to highlight the annual award’s 2026 shortlist.

In the video, viewers are introduced to Paisley Central Library, designed by Scottish studio Collective Architecture to serve as a welcoming community space with reading areas, event spaces and a study room.

According to studio director Ewan Imrie, Paisley Central Library was conceived as a civic space for “people to dwell”.

“The client was very clear from the outset that the building had to be as welcoming to all different types of people from all aspects of society,” Imrie said.

“Encouraging people to dwell was the most important thing and if they pick up a book and read it then that’s a bonus.”

Established in 2002, the Andrew Doolan Best Building in Scotland Award is an annual award that highlights Scottish projects, with this year’s winner set to be announced on 26 November.

Comprising three reuse projects, RIAS said this year’s shortlisted projects tap into “important conversations around community, collaboration, retrofit and reuse”. Shortlisted alongside the Paisley Central Library is the Edinburgh Futures Institute by Bennetts Associates and the Tarlair Outdoor Pool Pavilion by Studio Octopi.

Last year’s winner was a public park renewed by studios Stallan-Brand and LDA Design.

The film and photography is courtesy of Jim Stephenson.

The Sky Today on Friday, November 6: Nab the North America Nebula

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

As fall gets into full swing, we are seeing less of the summertime stars. By 6 P.M. local time, the Summer Triangle — which flies high overhead on short summer nights — is now starting to set in the west. Lowest of its three points is Vega in Lyra; to Vega’s upper left is Altair in Aquila, while Deneb in Cygnus is almost directly above Vega and the highest of the three.

With no Moon in the sky, let’s use Deneb to find a favorite deep-sky object: NGC 7000, the North America Nebula. It’s a great small scope target, and its location just 3° east of Deneb makes it easy to center in the eyepiece. However, this huge emission nebula stretches about 2° across, so note that it requires a larger field of view to capture all in one go. It definitely benefits from a nebula filter if you have one.

On the sky, the nebula’s western side looks like the eastern coast of the continent for which it is named, and vice versa. The curve that mimics the Gulf of Mexico is particularly prominent, so start there if you’re having trouble seeing the shape. Look also to NGC 7000’s west — across a dark gulf, you’ll see a fainter, more compact patch of nebulosity. This is IC 5067/5070, also known as the Pelican Nebula.

Sunrise: 6:35 A.M.Sunset: 4:52 P.M.Moonrise: 3:56 A.M.Moonset: 3:16 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (5%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

InfoQ Homepage News GitHub Actions Lockfiles Reach GA to Secure Workflow Dependencies

Nov 06, 2026 3 min read

GitHub has made workflow dependency lockfiles for GitHub Actions generally available on October 28, 2026, two weeks after the feature entered public preview. Workflows can maintain clear tag and branch references. The runner executes the specific commits listed in the .github/workflows/actions.lock file, even if those references change later. The feature is available on GitHub Enterprise Cloud, Enterprise Cloud with data residency, Team, and Free plans. The GA release includes the capabilities from the preview.

The change targets a long-standing weakness in how Actions resolves dependencies. A reference like actions/checkout@v4 or a branch name can change. This means a hacked action can add bad code to a pipeline without altering the repository using it. Teams can pin to full commit SHAs, but this reduces readability. It also leaves transitive dependencies, like those from composite actions, unpinned. Lockfiles address both gaps by recording commits for direct and transitive dependencies.

Tooling comes through the gh actions-lock extension for the GitHub CLI, hosted in the gh-actions-lock repository, which generates and maintains the lockfile. Adoption follows the workflow. Once a workflow is onboarded, every dependency needs a lockfile entry. The Actions runtime then runs the recorded commit, no matter where the tag or branch points. This lets teams migrate incrementally rather than converting an entire organization at once. The extension has a verification mode. It checks if the lockfile is up to date without changing any files. This way, it can act as a CI gate to spot differences between workflow files and the lockfile.

Dependabot integration addresses the usual cost of pinning, which is stale pins. When Dependabot opens a version update for an action in an onboarded workflow, it also updates the matching lockfile entry. Both changes appear in the same pull request, so the workflow reference and locked commit stay in sync. GitHub says this works without additional configuration. For governance, enterprise, organization, and repository admins can enable a "Require lockfile" policy. This policy helps enforce adoption at the relevant scope.

GitHub is explicit about the limits of the mechanism. A lockfile guarantees that runs use the commits you locked, but it does not determine whether those commits are safe, and the announcement advises reviewing dependencies before locking them. The security value therefore depends on the review that happens when an entry is created or updated. In practice, the Dependabot pull request becomes the review point, and teams that auto-merge those updates will carry any malicious change straight into the lockfile.

The release arrives as GitHub tightens other Actions defaults. A November 2 changelog entry announced that pull_request_target will be blocked by default starting December 2, another change aimed at limiting how untrusted code reaches privileged workflow contexts.

For teams evaluating adoption, setup instructions are in the GitHub Docs page on workflow dependency lockfiles. A reasonable starting point is to onboard a few high-privilege workflows, such as release or deployment pipelines, add the staleness check to CI, and confirm that Dependabot's combined pull requests fit existing review practices before enabling the enterprise-wide policy.

Competing platforms have not announced a native equivalent so far. GitLab's pipeline security guidance advises users to manually pin includes and components to commit SHAs. Users should also pin images to digests. After the March 2026 supply chain incidents, GitLab introduced open-source pipeline execution policies. These policies focus on blocking known attack patterns instead of creating a dependency manifest. Microsoft's Zero Trust guidance for Azure Pipelines also suggests using SHA pinning. It advises limiting Marketplace extensions too.

CircleCI orbs use a central semantic-versioning registry instead of commit hashes. This approach keeps the registry immutable. Teams on these platforms often use cross-platform tools. For example, they might use pinner to pin tags to SHAs and add a CI verification step. They may also rely on scanners like ActionSieve to flag unpinned references.

These tools pin the reference written in the pipeline file but do not resolve transitive dependencies, which is the gap GitHub's lockfile design addresses. As with GitHub's feature, none of them judges whether a pinned dependency is trustworthy, so the review step that precedes locking remains the control that matters.

InfoQ Homepage News Vault Fork OpenBao Adds External Keys and Quantum-Ready TLS

Nov 06, 2026 4 min read

OpenBao has released version 2.7, adding read scaling for PostgreSQL-backed deployments, support for external hardware security module (HSM) and key management service (KMS) keys, and post-quantum cryptographic algorithms. The release also introduces control groups for human review of sensitive operations and new headers intended to keep requests consistent across load-balanced OpenBao nodes.

OpenBao began as a community fork of HashiCorp Vault after HashiCorp changed Vault's licence from the Mozilla Public License to the Business Source License in 2023. The project is now governed under the Linux Foundation's Open Source Security Foundation and is distributed under MPL 2.0, an Open Source Initiative-approved licence. Its stated aim is to provide a secrets manager under open governance rather than control by a single vendor.

The fork was both ideological and commercial. Some users objected to the loss of an OSI-approved licence and to the possibility that a vendor could change the terms governing infrastructure they had already adopted. Others saw a practical opportunity: Vault had placed capabilities such as namespaces, HSM integration and advanced scalability behind enterprise licences, while an open fork could make comparable functionality available without those restrictions. The move followed the wider pattern that produced OpenTofu from Terraform and Valkey from Redis.

OpenBao has developed well beyond a simple continuation of Vault Community Edition. Since the fork, the project has added namespaces for multi-tenant isolation, plugin-based auto-unseal integrations, and read scalability for its integrated Raft storage backend. Version 2.7 extends that scaling work to PostgreSQL, allowing standby OpenBao instances using that backend to serve reads in a similar way to Raft-based deployments.

Alex Scheel announced the release for the OpenBao project, writing that it adds "PostgreSQL horizontal scalability, External HSM and KMS-Backed Keys support, and Post-Quantum Cryptography". He also noted that the release arrived after a shorter development cycle than the previous one and included "many substantial features and several major security patches".

The external keys feature lets the PKI and Transit secrets engines use key material held in an HSM or KMS rather than storing it inside OpenBao. Support is delivered through third-party plugins, building on the auto-unseal plugin work in the previous release. This should appeal to organisations that must keep root certification authority keys or other high-value material under hardware control.

The post-quantum work adds ML-DSA and ML-KEM to TLS listeners, certificate authentication, PKI and Transit. Operators can therefore use post-quantum signatures and key exchange in relevant parts of the platform. The change does not by itself make a deployment quantum-safe: clients, certificate authorities and surrounding systems must also support the chosen algorithms. It does, however, give OpenBao users a route to begin testing hybrid and post-quantum configurations.

Control groups add an approval workflow to OpenBao. A new control_group stanza in an ACL policy can require a second party to review access to specified paths. This is aimed at high-risk operations where a single authenticated identity should not be able to act alone.

The release also includes breaking changes. The separate HSM distribution has been discontinued because the PKCS#11 plugin is now externalised. Vendor-specific built-in auto-unseal mechanisms for Alibaba Cloud, AWS, Azure, Google Cloud and Oracle Cloud have also moved to plugins. Kerberos, LDAP and RADIUS authentication, along with the LDAP secrets engine, have moved into the openbao-plugins repository. The file storage backend has been removed; the project had never recommended it for production because it lacks high availability and transactions.

Luca Berton, who interviewed Scheel at Open Source Summit Europe, wrote that external keys allow operators to "run your PKI root backed by an HSM" with key material comparable to Vault Enterprise managed keys. He also highlighted the importance of post-quantum support before government transitions expected around 2030, citing the risk that encrypted traffic recorded today could be decrypted in the future.

Commercial support is also becoming part of the OpenBao ecosystem. ControlPlane launched ControlPlane Enterprise for OpenBao in March, offering security hardening, supply-chain assurance, rapid CVE remediation, optional FIPS 140-3 compliance support and 24/7 technical support. That offering illustrates how an open-source project can remain freely licensed while vendors compete on operational assurance. ControlPlane positions the product as a supported path for organisations that want OpenBao's licence and governance model but still need enterprise-style operational commitments. Its materials also contrast OpenBao's MPL 2.0 licence with Vault's BSL 1.1 licence and note that OpenBao retains API compatibility with Vault for many use cases.

Secrets management being a foundational dependency has been covered in InfoQ recently. We previously reported on Vault 2.0 and IBM's support model following its acquisition of HashiCorp, as well as on the broader pattern of community forks preserving open standards after vendor licence changes. OpenBao's progress suggests that the fork is becoming a credible alternative rather than merely a protest project.

OpenBao version 2.8 is expected to focus more on security and stability, with likely improvements to authentication. For teams evaluating a migration, the immediate questions are less about individual features than about operational maturity: plugin lifecycle management, PostgreSQL high-availability configuration, post-quantum client compatibility and the availability of support for production incidents.

InfoQ Homepage Articles Identity as an Architectural Concern: Enterprise IAM Beyond Authentication

Nov 06, 2026 12 min read

For a single application, identity is mostly a vendor decision. You pick an identity provider, wire up SSO, validate tokens at the edge, and move on with the features. For one application and one team, that's the right call. It stops being enough as the organization grows. Login keeps working fine. Everything after that gets difficult: who approved a given access and whether it should still exist when an auditor asks about it months later.

This article draws on building and operating an identity and access management platform in regulated financial services, where a wrong entitlement becomes an audit finding. My argument is that at enterprise scale, identity has to be treated as part of the architecture. Teams that add it late tend to find it conflicts with decisions they've already locked in. I'll assume you have experience shipping OAuth 2.0 or SSO. What follows covers the design problems that appear once many services, and many owners, are involved.

Identity belongs in architecture discussions because it constrains decisions that look unrelated. The data model is where this shows up first. Once you have to explain and audit access, "who can touch this resource" needs to be a relationship you can query with history, because auditors ask what was true six months ago as well as what is true today. Service boundaries and event flows raise their own problems; each gets its own section below.

Explore how Azul helps Java teams reduce JVM vulnerability risk, strengthen application security, and prepare their Java environments for agentic AI. Discover practical strategies for securing Java workloads while maintaining performance and reliability. Learn more.

Regulation adds another constraint. Here's a useful test: Could you answer "Who had access to this system on 14 March?" from your data model alone, without grepping logs across several tools? If not, an audit wasn't part of the design.

A security review can catch a missing check or a misconfigured scope, but it can't retrofit a schema that was never built to answer that question. The cheapest way to pass that test is to record every grant, revocation, and attestation as an event with a timestamp and an actor from the start. Then the auditor's question is one query against the event log.

The Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) model is used by default and is usually sufficient. It strains when your questions shift from "Does this user have this role?" to how things relate to each other. Our entities are people, the service accounts they own, and the accesses those accounts hold. The questions we kept asking were: "Who owns this account?", "Is this access still valid?", and "What does this person control?"

All three are relationship questions. Ownership is a relationship. An access has a validity window and an attestation history attached to it. A person who leaves or changes roles pulls a set of owned accounts along behind them. In a role table, you rebuild each of those links with joins every time you ask. In a graph, each link is an edge and the query walks them.

That's why our backend models these relationships in Neo4j, queried through GraphQL. GraphQL fits because a query's nesting mirrors the traversal: this account, then its owner, and then its access. A caller asks for exactly the slice of the graph it needs, from an account down to its valid accesses. Nobody handwrites traversal logic or over-fetches a large object and filters it in memory. Figure 1 shows the shape of the underlying model.

Figure 1. Ownership-and-access graph modeled in Neo4j, showing how flat RBAC breaks down when access needs expiration and nuance. (Image source: created by the author.)

Before the query, here is the model it runs against. There are three kinds of nodes: Person, ServiceAccount, and Resource. There are two associations between them. First, a person OWNS a service account, while second, a service account HAS_ACCESS to a resource. The association, HAS_ACCESS, possesses some properties such as expiration, the date for when the access will be no longer valid, and lastAttested, which displays the last date when the access was attested by the owner. Validity is an attribute of the edge rather than of any node since it refers to the particular combination of the account and resource nodes.

This query, as shown below, poses a question we frequently ask ourselves: Who is the owner of this account and which of its accesses are currently valid? Looking through the query from the top, the first MATCH gets the account based on its ID and follows the OWNS relationship until it reaches the owner. The next OPTIONAL MATCH follows the HAS_ACCESS relationship towards each of the resources, and the WHERE clause appended to it filters out any access where the expiry date has expired. Because it is an OPTIONAL MATCH, an account having no accesses left will yield its owner anyway. Finally, the RETURN clause yields the owner’s name along with all the accesses left with their resources, expiry dates, and last attested date in a single array.

Written as SQL against role tables, the same question needs every link lined up by hand:

Even here, it's easy to get it wrong. Put the expiry check in the WHERE clause instead of the join, and accounts with no valid grants vanish from the result, owner and all. The query works, but it still leaves three problems out of view. Ownership is a single join in this example, but ownership can be delegated, and once it can, that join becomes a walk up a chain of unknown length. In the graph, the same chain is a variable-length path in one pattern. The expiry check also has to be repeated in every query that touches grants. Any query that leaves it out returns access that should have been removed. In the graph, the check sits inside the traversal, so a consumer can't skip it by accident. Finally, when a person leaves, the accounts they own aren't in front of you, and you have to go looking for them. In the graph they're one hop away, because "owns" is an edge. Both queries are simplified from what we run in production.

But Neo4j has inherent costs. It's one more datastore to operate, and traversal performance depends heavily on how you model things. A badly shaped graph can be slower than the join it replaced. It was still worth it for us, because ownership and validity are things we traverse constantly. The flat version pushes the hard part onto whoever writes the next query.

Comparing everything in the platform, the service accounts took the most governance work. Every service account is owned by a real person: Roughly eighty thousand people review, rotate, and vault the accounts they own. An account’s safety depends mostly on that owner: whether they're still engaged and whether they've changed teams or left the company. An account whose owner has moved on is exactly the stale, over-privileged credential that nobody notices until it's misused.

Before the rebuild, the three things an owner had to do (attestation, rotation, and vaulting) lived in three separate tools. An owner had to remember which tool did what. That's a small annoyance for someone who does it daily. For someone who does it twice a year, or who inherited an account when a colleague left, it's a real failure mode.

The rebuild put all three owner requirements into a single workflow, so an owner sees one screen with what they own and what's due. Getting there took more than wiring screens together. Each tool had been built with its own assumptions about schedules and ownership, and once they shared a workflow, those assumptions had to be reconciled. Figure 2 shows the revocation workflow before and after the consolidation: Three separate tools, with revocation taking about three days, became one unified workflow that revokes in under a day.

Figure 2. Revocation workflow before and after tool consolidation. (Image source: created by author.)

In enterprise identity work, the risky integrations are usually the ones where your system depends on a system you don't govern. For us, that was vaulting. We already handled attestation and rotation well before the rebuild. We had written that code ourselves, so we knew how it failed. Vaulting ran in a separate tool, owned by another team, on infrastructure we couldn't change. Folding it into our workflow meant a critical control now depended on their release schedule and their outages.

The hard part was deciding what our workflow should believe when their API didn't answer, or answered slowly, or answered after our call had already timed out. Those are not edge cases when you're doing this for tens of thousands of owners. They happen every day, somewhere. So we made the integration "fail closed". If the vault was unreachable, the workflow didn't report the step as complete, and an owner was never told a credential was vaulted when it wasn't. That sounds obvious on paper. In practice it meant our side couldn't treat "we sent the request" as success. We had to confirm the vault's own state, reconcile it against ours, and treat any gap between the two as something our side resolves.

If every service makes its own access decisions, authorization logic ends up scattered across dozens of codebases, where nobody can audit it. When a rule changes, you hunt for it through every service that has implemented its own version, and you can miss one. What works is letting identity context travel across boundaries while the decision stays in a dedicated policy decision point. Identity propagates through scoped claims using OAuth 2.0 token exchange, so a service acting for a caller gets a narrowed token instead of reusing a broad one.

The controller asks, and the policy decision point decides as shown in the following code snippet:

The controller never sees the rules, so changing a rule requires changing the PDP and nothing else.

Across a service hop, the incoming token is exchanged for a scoped one before the downstream call:

The token for each hop records its audience and scope, so the audit log shows what every call was for.

Entitlement changes propagate as events using Kafka. A grant, a revocation, or a change of ownership becomes a message that interested services consume. That's a sensible design for a distributed system and it indicates revocation is eventually consistent. Access stops once consumers process the event and invalidate any cached decisions, which happens some time after the revocation is recorded. That gap should be measured and capped before anyone describes revocation to a security stakeholder.

A consumer handling a revocation invalidates the cached decision, so the next check re-resolves from the source of truth:

Events can be lost, so the cache also has a time limit. A cached decision is served only while it's younger than a maximum age. After that, it's re-resolved from the source of truth whether or not an event arrived. That maximum age is the worst case scenario for a dropped event. Before we relied on the design, I simulated the consumer and cache to confirm that a missed event never kept access live past it.

Choosing the maximum age involves a real trade-off. A short maximum age causes stale access to expire quickly, which is what security wants, but every expiry sends another check back to the source of truth. At our volume that load isn't free. A long maximum age is easy on the system and worse for revocation, because a dropped event has longer to matter. You pick a number you can live with on both sides, then state it plainly: A lost event leaves access live for at most that long. That's a sentence a security team can plan around.

The cache window was the small part, though. The larger delay was procedural. Before the rebuild, fully revoking access took around three days, most of the time being spent on steps a person had to complete in separate tools. With a single workflow, access revocation takes under a day.

For a single app with a few roles and one team, you can skip all of this. Flat RBAC in your primary database is the right choice, and Neo4j, Kafka, and a policy layer would be over-engineering you'll regret maintaining. The threshold is organizational: many services making access decisions, service accounts that outnumber and outlive the people minding them, ownership that has to be re-verified, and an audit obligation with teeth. Here's how I would weigh each piece:

Authorization is moving out of individual services and into policy engines that can be tested on their own. For service accounts, the zero-trust version of this is continuous verification: an account re-proves its entitlements on a schedule instead of keeping them by default. Our service account rebuild was a manual version of the same idea, because attestation is reverification with a human in the loop. The interesting question is how much of that human step can be made automatic without losing the accountability that a named owner provides.
