UK Warns Its Critical Infrastructure Faces Grave Risk

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Britain's new Labour-led government is making a concerted push to get ahead of the AI risks to its national networks and the industrial complex.

At various levels of the United Kingdom government, officials are warning about the threat of cyberattacks against its critical infrastructure.

His Majesty's Government broadcasted its cybersecurity agenda on Nov. 3, releasing a variety of policy papers, an annual report from its lead cybersecurity agency, and related announcements pertaining to the security of small and medium-sized businesses (SMBs), supply chain vendors, critical national infrastructure (CNI), and more. These items all followed from an updated Autumn Budget 2026, released last week, which provisioned nearly two hundred billion pounds for AI-driven government cybersecurity projects.

Amid all of the practical policy updates, the headline quote of the day came from the state's National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), which assessed that it's "highly likely that between now and 2028, AI‑powered malware will have been used in a ransomware attack on UK CNI." It also labeled "highly likely" the prospect that "China, Russia, and other nation states will routinely use AI cyber to achieve their geopolitical goals" in the country.

Related:Third Serengeti Sweep Tops 1,400 Arrests as Investigations Mature

In a new report, Manchester-based NCC Group polled Members of Parliament (MPs) about the state of the UK's CNI cybersecurity posture. About half (51%) of MPs reported that their areas of the country are "not keeping pace" with the threats to its infrastructure. Only 22% expressed confidence in their country's posture. Asked about the nation's ability to fight off a major attack against its CNI, 47% believe that it's unprepared, whereas 41% feel it is.

The survey data is best read as aspirational, not prescriptive. There's little evidence that the UK is less prepared than other nations in addressing threats to its critical infrastructure, or that politicians would be able to tell one way or the other. Leaders are simply aware of the magnitude of what faces them.

"The challenge isn't a lack of government action, it’s proof that action translates into resilience," says Katharina Sommer, head of government affairs at NCC Group. "There's still no tested blueprint for coordinating cyber response effectively across government and industry, and that’s not a problem unique to the UK. The test for critical infrastructure operators is whether they can demonstrate, before an attack hits, that they can sustain essential services, recover quickly and maintain public confidence," which remains an open question.

A clearer message came through on Tuesday, from the experts at the NCSC. Using a scale in which "likely" accords with roughly 55 to 75% odds, "highly likely" with 80% to 90% odds, and "almost certain" approaching 100%, it bet that it is:

Related:UAE Transitions 50% of Government Services to Agentic AI

Likely that threat actors will use AI cyber tools to needle vulnerabilities in CNI

Highly likely that nation-states will use AI to increase the speed and sophistication of their cyberattacks, in service of their geopolitical goals in the UK; in particular, the agency identified China as the foremost player in terms of intent and capability to hit CNI and other strategic targets

Almost certain that "many systems will remain vulnerable" to AI-empowered attacks

The NCSC also drew out some future scenarios. Optimistically, it acknowledged that AI-driven cyber threat development might slow, allowing a window of time for the UK to catch up with the problem. Pessimistically, it painted a picture in which AI threats totally outpace the UK's ability to keep up.

"The situation is likely to be worse than is currently known, with many variables and unknowns still to be discovered," according to the report.

The message out of the UK government on Tuesday was, more or less: we're trying to get ahead of this.

In a policy paper, the government acknowledged that 2018's Network and Information Systems (NIS) Regulations — the UK's only multisector cybersecurity law — is "out of date and insufficient to tackle the threats we face." In a speech announcing its Annual Review 2026, NCSC CEO Richard Horne called the law built to replace NIS 2018 — November 2025's Cyber Security and Resilience Bill — "a vital step forward." The updated bill brings data centers, managed service providers (MSPs), and specific "critical suppliers" into the fold, and introduces stricter breach reporting requirements and hefty fines for negligence.

Related:Australian Gov't Weighs Mandatory AI Incident Reporting

In a separate policy paper concerning the same bill, the government stated its intention to introduce a new vendor risk management framework focused specifically on the "national security risks posed by vendors" connected to essential service providers. And in yet a third, related paper, the government described how the Cyber Security and Resilience Bill will be used to account for agentic "loss of control" incidents, in the fashion of this summer's AI industry lab leaks. Projected future measures include:

Security and resilience requirements to prevent loss of control incidents

Empowering ministers to perform postmortems

The bill is currently passing through Parliament.

Besides this one bill, the UK government also announced £2.4 billion in subsidies to help micro-, small, and medium-sized businesses attain "Cyber Essentials" certifications. All of this week's announcements come on the heels of an Autumn Budget last week, which arranged for tens of billions of pounds each to fund, among other things:

A National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability (NERV) Centre, for vulnerability discovery across the country, including CNI

A National Cyber Shield, for training AI agents to automate cyber defense

A separate National Agentic Security Unit for fighting rogue AI agents

"Increasing the funding and focus on cybersecurity to help keep the UK secure is very welcome," says Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence for KnowBe4. At the same time, he adds, "the main challenge I see is scale."

"£154 million for cyber-defenses is a good step," he believes, "but the NCSC estimates cyberattacks cost UK organizations £14.7 billion a year."

Dark Reading asked cybersecurity experts what governments like the UK's need to do to prevent future worst case scenarios. They consistently emphasized the same point:

"AI did not create new weaknesses, it revealed them. Most incidents still start through the same familiar routes, such as phishing, stolen credentials and the traditional kill chain paths attackers have relied on for years. The key to stopping these threats is, surprisingly, not exciting: it's the fundamentals," Chapman argues.

The fundamentals for Graham Taylor CBE, director of defense strategy for Northern Europe at OPSWAT, starts with protecting the edge. "Too many critical sites rely on firewalls and VPNs for connections that only need to send data one way. Where a facility only exports monitoring data, a hardware-enforced data diode removes the return path entirely, so neither a human attacker nor AI can use that link to send commands into the facility," he says.

Jacob Krell, Suzu Labs senior director for secure AI solutions and cybersecurity, emphasizes network segmentation "built like a boat." AI is going to make the perimeter easier to breach, he says, "and it cuts the time to objective once an attacker is inside. Critical infrastructure is notoriously vulnerable here because operators are hesitant to patch and test systems, and their caution is rational, a bad update takes a plant offline. So the perimeter fails more often and the interior offers nothing to slow lateral movement."

John Strand, owner of Black Hills Information Security, tosses out a less conventional suggestion for fighting off AI threats to CNI. "If AI gives attackers the ability to do a tremendous amount of work very quickly, defenders have an opportunity to present them with large numbers of decoys to investigate. We need creative ways to make those decoys look realistic and dynamic. The more time attacking AI agents spend interacting with decoys, the less time they spend attacking real systems," he argues.

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

In Zagreb, the company that owns the robotaxis chose the aggregator on day one

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On 26 March 2026 Uber, Verne and Pony.ai announced a three-way deal to bring robotaxis to Europe. Pony.ai supplies the autonomous driving, Croatian company Verne buys the cars and keeps them on the road, Uber brings the network. That same announcement already stated that rides would be bookable "via Uber as well as through Verne's app", and that Uber "intends to invest an undisclosed amount into Verne".

Two weeks later, on 8 April, Europe's first commercial robotaxi service started in Zagreb: Verne's app, about ninety square kilometres of the city including the airport, from seven in the morning to nine at night.

On 19 August the rides also appeared on Uber's app, with a licensed operator on board behind the wheel, Verne acting as fleet owner and service operator. On 10 September the first rides with nobody at the wheel began, on the twenty-two kilometres to the airport: test rides for invited passengers, while the commercial service kept the operator on board. On 7 October the investment was confirmed by Verne's chief executive, Marko Pejković: "I can confirm, yes, Uber has invested in Verne." The size of the stake was not disclosed.

The press covered the machine. For anyone coordinating a fleet, the thing to look at is different, and it is not an ambush: it is a choice. Europe's most advanced operator, the one that actually owns the driverless cars and has its own app, decided from day one to also go through the aggregator's app, and to let the aggregator into its capital.

If that is what the company with the robotaxis does, it is worth asking what a dispatch company will do: one with the shifts, the licences and thirty years of customers, but without a list to restart from.

You do not need to wait for robotaxis to see how this ends. It has already happened twice, with human drivers.

August 2020: Uber buys Autocab, the British company that supplies booking and dispatch software to private hire operators, which is to say to its own competitors. On 29 March 2021 the UK competition authority closed its inquiry finding "no competition concerns", and the deal completed in April.

It is worth reading why it closed. The authority had considered exactly the data question, whether operators might be pushed into handing data to Uber, and set it aside by observing that there are "other credible suppliers" those operators "could switch" to if the service worsened or if data were demanded. In other words: in the authority's reasoning, the protection is not a promise, it is being able to leave.

The operators' main question at the time was the same one: what happens to our data. Autocab answered that it would handle them as always, without sharing. No abuse has emerged since, and that is not the point. The point is that the answer was a statement and not a clause, while the real guarantee had already been named by the regulator: the ability to walk away.

April 2025: Lyft buys FreeNow for 175 million euros. It was not buying cars. Ninety per cent of the gross bookings on that platform in 2024 came from taxis, which is to say from other people's vehicles and licences.

In both cases whoever was driving stayed at the wheel. What changed is who stands on the other side: in the first the supplier became the competitor, in the second the app changed owner.

In Italy the instinctive answer is that none of this applies yet, since the road code defines a vehicle as a machine "driven by a human" and a car that drives itself does not fit. Testing is nonetheless governed by the 2018 Smart Road decree.

But those rules defend the licence, not the relationship with the person getting into the car. No law obliges a passenger to call one number rather than another. Customers go where booking is easier, and they do not ask anyone's permission.

Original (Italian): A Zagabria anche l'operatore locale passa da Uber

Disclosure: I build UrbanGo, dispatch software for licensed taxi and private-hire operators. Everything above is sourced; this paragraph is the part where I sell something.

Stop Stale Editors Overwriting Changes with If-Match

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two people open the same settings page. One changes the title; the other saves a description a minute later. Both receive a successful response, but the second save restores the old title.

The second person did not deliberately undo anything. Their editor submitted an old copy of the document. If your API accepts every replacement unconditionally, the most recent request wins even when it contains older information.

For an HTTP API, a useful defence is a strong ETag paired with If-Match. The client sends the version it edited; the server applies the change only if that version is still current. A mismatch normally produces 412 Precondition Failed, giving the editor a chance to reconcile instead of silently overwriting.

Keep the quoted validator with the loaded document. When saving, send it unchanged:

If another editor has already produced version 18, the condition is false. RFC 9110's If-Match rules define this conditional request behaviour. The header uses strong comparison: a weak tag such as W/"settings-17" cannot satisfy it. See Mozilla's If-Match reference.

Treat the ETag as an opaque value. Clients should not increment its number or reconstruct it from a timestamp. The server owns its meaning, including which representation it identifies.

This small model demonstrates the save rule. Save it as conditional-save.mjs and run node conditional-save.mjs with a current Node.js release. It uses only the built-in assertion module:

Ben's description is still in his local editor. It was never accepted by the server. That is the point at which the interface should offer a comparison, not announce that everything has been saved.

A server implementation can still lose updates if it checks the version, yields to another request, and later writes without a condition. Comparing the tag in one database query and performing an unconditional update in another recreates the race.

For a versioned row, the core operation might be:

Interpret the affected-row count and distinguish a missing resource from a stale version according to your API contract. Authenticate and authorise the caller independently. For an ETag representing several rows, the transaction must cover every change contributing to that representation.

This SQL is an implementation sketch, not a complete HTTP validator parser. A production endpoint must also handle required-header policy, validation, representation variants and its database's transaction behaviour.

On 412, keep the unsaved changes, fetch the latest document and show what changed. Automatically replacing the ETag and resubmitting the old whole document defeats the protection. A field-by-field merge can help, but overlapping edits still need a deliberate decision.

If-Match: * is not equivalent to a version-specific tag: it checks for an existing representation, not whether the editor saw the current one.

Also separate concurrency from transport uncertainty. If a response disappears after a successful save, the caller needs to discover the outcome; retry design is a separate concern. Jitsumi's explanation of API idempotency and uncertain retries works through that boundary.

For caching, the related validator question is usually whether a stored representation can be reused. Jitsumi's ETag and Cache-Control example covers that read path. The save path here asks a different question: is this still the version the editor is entitled to replace?

The Biggest Stock Winners and Losers From the Midterm Elections

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The market historically performs well after midterm elections, but some stocks appear to be clear winners and losers following the results.

The midterm elections shifted the political landscape in the U.S., and, regardless of anyone's politics, that will impact some industries and stocks in a meaningful way going forward. Stocks have historically performed well following the midterm elections, regardless of which party wins. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -4.36%) has climbed from November to November 95% of the time since 1938, so now should still be a good time to buy stocks in general.

However, let's look at some stocks set to benefit from the election results and others that could be hurt.

Tariffs have been a bane to the retail sector, and the election results give retailers hope that there could be a future shift in policy. This isn't likely to happen right away and may not take hold until after the 2028 elections. However, the market tends to be forward-looking and already anticipating better days for retailers. Here are three retail-oriented stocks that could benefit.

Amazon (AMZN -2.19%) is the largest e-commerce company in both the U.S. and the world. The company's logistics network gives it a significant advantage, and it has been seeing strong operating leverage from its investments in AI and robotics. Meanwhile, any removal of tariffs should be a big boost to its retail operations, as it would increase consumer demand and help expand margins on the goods it sells.

Interestingly, Amazon's AWS unit is also viewed as a winner. While the elections were seen as a backlash against the AI data center build-out, this is expected to hurt neoclouds and benefit the big cloud providers. The reason is that the big cloud computing companies are generally better positioned to navigate regulatory hurdles, given their established relationships with utilities, regulators, and local governments, as well as their better financial resources and ability to secure long-term power agreements.

Shopify (SHOP -6.06%) has become the e-commerce software company retailers are increasingly turning to in an effort to take on Amazon. The stronger its retail customers are and the better they are performing, the more it benefits Shopify, which gets a revenue cut through its Shopify Payments solution.

The company also appears to be one of the biggest potential AI winners. This is a top stock to own if tariff pressures ease and consumer spending strengthens.

e.l.f. Beauty (ELF -3.33%) was one of the hardest-hit brands from tariffs, given most of its products are manufactured in China. Meanwhile, a $1 price increase for its namesake brand impacted its sales growth. As such, a rollback in tariffs could be a huge benefit to the company.

Meanwhile, the company's acquisition of Hailey Bieber's Rhode brand is proving to be a huge winner. The company is expanding Rhode's product assortment and distribution, which is leading to strong growth. A tariff-reduction tailwind would only add to its growth story.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -9.05%) has become one of the largest neoclouds, with aggressive goals to expand its computing power capacity. Tougher permitting could put a real dent in its ability to grow as quickly as it would like, with permitting delays leading to lost revenue. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, for instance, specifically ran on limiting data center growth.

Meanwhile, it could also hamper SpaceX's ability to launch another U.S. mobile provider and complicate a potential merger with Tesla.

On the upside, if SpaceX can overcome technical hurdles and costs come down, its orbital data center ambitions could become a real advantage in a tougher terrestrial data center permitting environment. Unfortunately, the same can't be said for other neoclouds like CoreWeave (CRWV -9.83%), Iren (IREN -16.95%), and Nebius (NBIS -14.95%).

Palantir Technologies (PLTR -9.73%) has become one of the most important AI software-as-a-service (SaaS) companies on the planet, with its solution akin to an AI operating system that makes AI more useful in the real world. Its growth has been outstanding, driven by U.S. commercial customers, but the U.S. government remains its largest client.

The company's U.S. government growth has been strong, but a potential shift in administration could slow it. Given the stock's hefty valuation, any slowdown in growth could hurt the stock. However, I'd likely view any significant dip as a buying opportunity.

Geoffrey Seiler has positions in Amazon and e.l.f. Beauty. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Palantir Technologies, and Shopify. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies and e.l.f. Beauty. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Diamonds are forever - phoenix club seek FA Cup upset

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The notion of your football club ceasing to exist is unthinkable for many fans, but for supporters of Rushden & Diamonds it became a sad reality in 2011.

The Northamptonshire-based side, backed by the late Max Griggs, rocketed through non-league in the 1990s and in 2003 reached the third tier of English football before financial issues after he stepped away ultimately saw them fold.

A community lost its focal point, and within years their Nene Park stadium had been razed to the ground.

"It was heartbreaking - we knew we'd been robbed." reflected Rob Usher, the chairman of phoenix side AFC Rushden & Diamonds, who will make their FA Cup first-round debut this weekend.

"I think it was that anger that probably fuelled the fans saying 'no, we don't accept it. We're not gone, we're not done and we want our club back."

Some 2,000 travelling fans will make their way to see the now eighth-tier Diamonds take on League One side Burton Albion on Saturday - a bittersweet reminder of days the supporters of the original club could have once taken for granted.

Usher, like many of those involved with the Northern Premier League Division One Midlands side, has followed both incarnations of the club - firstly as a fan, but also as a groundsman and turnstile operator before becoming chairman in 2022.

"My first game was an FA Cup tie against Shrewsbury Town in 1998. It was the year we ended up playing Leeds United in the third round." he said.

"Rushden & Diamonds gave us some great times. Winning leagues, the Duane Darby's, the Onandi Lowe's of our past. But I tell everyone I've never traded for what we have with AFC Rushden & Diamonds.

"This is a community, this is an army of people who turn up and give their time and their money, their care, their devotion... not because they want the club to exist, but because they want the club to thrive. That's why it's so special".

Diamonds chairman Rob Usher said the club was at the heart of its community [James Burridge/BBC]

Diamonds, who now play their home games at Hayden Road, started their journey to the first round proper with a 5-0 win at Kimberley Miners Welfare in the extra preliminary round on 8 August.

They have also beaten Long Eaton United, Desborough Town, Peterborough Sports, Braintree Town and Morecambe to reach this stage.

Along with Stafford Rangers, who host Grimsby Town on Sunday, they are the only side left in the competition to have played from the earliest round and have so far earned £23,194 from the FA's prize fund.

The winners of the tie at Burton's Pirelli Stadium will collect £47,750, with the losers taking £15,800.

Joining AFC Rushden & Diamonds' attempt to make more history will be Billy Sharp, the Championship's all-time record goalscorer, who has reversed his recent decision to retire.

The 40-year-old, best known for his spells with Sheffield United and Doncaster Rovers, scored his first nine professional goals while on loan from the Blades to the original Diamonds in 2005.

"They [Rushden & Diamonds] made me as a player," said Sharp, who will be targeting a landmark 300th goal while filling in for forward James Clifton who is serving a suspension for accumulated yellow cards.

Diamonds' FA Cup story too hard to turn down - Sharp

Retired Sheff Utd legend Sharp signs for Rushden

"I've stayed in contact with the club over last few years, I should've been here for a legends game and I couldn't make it. But I always look out for their results.

"Elliot [Sandy, the club's manager] asked me if I'd consider playing, I said let's see what happens v Morecambe. Within 20 minutes of the result he text me an eyes emoji.

"I just smiled and said give me a day or two. It just seemed right. The opportunity to score another goal, to play another game in the FA Cup. It's special. This club means a lot to me, it gave me my chance when I was a young kid. Hopefully we can cause a shock, if we get through to the next round James [Clifton] can get his chance."

Sam Brown has played 350 games for AFC Rushden & Diamonds and supported the original club [James Burridge/BBC]

Defender Sam Brown, who grew up watching Rushden & Diamonds on the terraces at Nene Park, remembers chanting Sharp's name and expects it to feel "weird" to now play alongside him.

Brown was involved in the phoenix club's inaugural season and has made 350 appearances across two spells.

"It's been a rollercoaster," he said. "It's a big journey and I'm glad to have been a part of it.

"The club is owned by the fans so it needs all the fans to pull together to be able to grow. They turn up every Saturday no matter where we are and it's nice to have a chance for them to enjoy a different kind of environment on Saturday."

This is AFC Rushden & Diamonds' first appearance in the FA Cup proper. Their predecessors, Rushden & Diamonds, last featured in 2010-11, losing 3-0 to Yeovil Town in the first round [Shaun Frankham]

While the travelling supporters will hope to see an upset against Burton, for many, the experience of bringing their community together for such an occasion will provide the most lasting memories.

Diamonds say they have put on six coaches "at cost price" to help transport fans to the game in east Staffordshire.

Many are part owners and volunteers, including husband and wife Teresa and David Hales who help look after the club's kit.

"It's brilliant, absolutely brilliant." said Teresa. "For a little club like us it's absolutely amazing. I'm just so proud of everybody. Players, volunteers, everybody.

"There have been times over the years where I've sat with my head in my hands thinking, 'oh God, what's going to happen next?' But we're on the way up. We're really on the way up."

Teresa (left) and David (centre) Hales are part owners of the club and volunteer as kit managers [James Burridge/BBC]

"It would be the shock of shocks," said club chairman Usher. "I've seen this squad this year pull off unbelievable things when the odds are against us, so I don't count us out.

"I've seen this club come back from death quite literally in 2011 and once again four years ago [when the phoenix club faced financial troubles].

"Diamonds are never done. Diamonds are forever."

Nene Park, the home of the original Rushden & Diamonds, was demolished in 2017 [Shutterstock]

Rushden & Diamonds were formed in 1992 following the merger of Rushden Town and Irthlingborough Diamonds.

The merger was the brainchild of Max Griggs, the owner of Northamptonshire-based Dr. Martens, with the aim of getting the single, larger club into the Football League.

The club reached the Conference, now known as the National League, in 1996 and realised Griggs' dream of EFL football in 2001 under the management of Brian Talbot.

In 2003, Diamonds were promoted as champions to Division Two, now known as League One, but Griggs' retirement as chairman signalled the beginning of their ultimate demise.

By 2006 they had been relegated back to non-league and mounting financial pressures saw them kicked out the Conference in 2011 before ultimately folding.

Rivals Kettering Town moved into the club's Nene Park stadium at Irthlingborough for a brief and ill-fated period between 2011 and 2012, before the ground was demolished in 2017.

Jaguars' point differential of +108 this season is 50 points better than any other team

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

No NFL team is dominating opponents like the Jacksonville Jaguars.

After blowing out the Ravens 33-10 on Thursday night, the Jaguars have now outscored their opponents by a cumulative score of 229-121 this season. That’s a point differential of +108, by far the best in the NFL.

The team with the second-best point differential in the NFL is the 49ers, at +58. The Jaguars’ point differential is 50 points better than the next-best team.

At 6-2, the Jaguars do not have the best record in the league, as the Bills, 49ers and Vikings are all 6-1. The 49ers are favored to beat the Raiders and improve to 7-1, and the Bills and Vikings play each other on Monday night, so the winner will be 7-1.

But at the midway point of the season, point differential is a better indication of a team’s quality, and a better predictor of a team’s future success, than win-loss record. That’s because with as small a sample size as half a season, a few plays in a few close games can skew the won-loss record, but a few plays don’t skew point differential. Historically, the teams that prove to be the best over the course of a season are the teams that win some blowouts, even if they also lose some close games.

Winning blowouts and losing close games is exactly what the Jaguars do. Both of the Jaguars’ losses were one-possession games that were tied in the fourth quarter, while the Jaguars’ wins have included some massive blowouts: The Jaguars’ 35-point win over the Colts was the biggest blowout of this NFL season, their 29-point win over the Patriots was the fourth-biggest blowout in the NFL this season, their 24-point win over the Browns was the seventh-biggest blowout in the NFL this season, and Thursday night’s 23-point win over the Ravens was the 11th-biggest blowout in the NFL this season.

The Jaguars have won four games by more than 21 points this season; no other team has more than one win by more than 21 points.

Jacksonville has a 3.5-game lead in the AFC South, and six of the Jaguars’ nine remaining games are against opponents with a losing record. They’re poised to run away with their division, perhaps earn the No. 1 seed in the AFC, and maybe blow some teams out in the playoffs just like they have in the regular season.

Tanks or drones? Defense earnings reveal a more complicated rearmament boom

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Drone technology and artificial intelligence are rapidly reshaping modern warfare, but the world's biggest defense contractors are finding that demand for tanks, fighter jets and other traditional military equipment remains strong.

Recent earnings from U.S. and European defense companies show conventional weapons continue to drive year-on-year growth, even alongside surging demand for advanced sensors, autonomous systems and counter-drone technology.

Rather than displacing traditional weapons manufacturers, the rise of drones and autonomous warfare is creating new business for many of the same companies.

From counter-drone defenses to advanced sensors integrated into tanks and fighter jets, the technologies transforming the battlefield are also changing, or even expanding, the markets served by legacy defense giants.

Lockheed Martin reported a 24% jump in revenue from its missiles business in the third quarter, while RTX secured multibillion-dollar contracts for established missile systems. In Europe, German defense supplier Renk saw orders for its military vehicle business surge 46% in the first nine months of the year.

Yet, the same earnings also show how the boundaries between legacy weapons and emerging defense technology are increasingly blurred.

German defense electronics company Hensoldt reported a 52% jump in revenue from its optronics business, with its chief financial officer telling CNBC that much of the growth came from conventional military equipment, including tanks and armored vehicles.

Meanwhile, Rheinmetall's air defense business, which includes counter-drone systems, reported 63% revenue growth, even as the wider company cut its full-year outlook, citing project delays.

The rise of drones is transforming traditional military platforms rather than replacing them, with tanks and other weapons increasingly integrated into connected, data-driven battlefield networks, according to Morningstar.

Over the past five years and since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, defense primes have seen their order books swell amid higher military spending among governments globally.

Yet, analysts mostly say that the rearmament boom is structural, and even an end to wars in Ukraine and Iran won't derail it. Crucially, Europe now has to play catch-up amid increasing uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees and Russia's next move, after decades of military underfunding.

However, concerns that valuations had run ahead of the industry's ability to deliver dented defense stocks this year amid several instances of project delays and contract cancellations.

Advancements in modern warfare and technology – as proven by Ukraine's use of drones on the front lines – further hit investor confidence in defense contractors amid worries that new technologies may displace demand for traditional military equipment.

Beyond the front line, NATO plans to invest more than $40 billion in counter-drone capabilities over the next five years, as Secretary General Mark Rutte warned that drones have fundamentally transformed modern warfare.

Even so, Hensoldt CFO Christian Ladurner told CNBC on Thursday that demand for traditional military equipment remains a key driver of growth.

Its fast-growing optronics business, which supplies optical and sensor technologies, is benefiting from European governments' efforts to replenish conventional military equipment, even as newer battlefield technologies attract increasing attention, Ladurner said.

He also highlighted growing demand for air defense systems, as militaries confront an expanding range of threats, from small drones to ballistic missiles, adding that the air defense market is "underinvested" despite growing by 20% a year.

Limits to Patriot interceptors produced by Lockheed Martin are being felt hard, not least in Ukraine, after the U.S. depleted much of its stockpile of the state-of-the-art system able to take down high-speed missiles in the Iran war.

More recently, defense stocks have come under pressure amid concerns that the Democrats' U.S. midterm victories could squeeze American military spending.

While the result has dampened expectations for defense spending, with Democratic control of Congress making a proposed $1.5 trillion budget unlikely and potentially weighing on military spending growth through 2029, nothing has materially changed the geopolitical calculations, TD Cowen analyst Roman Schweizer told CNBC's "Morning Call" on Thursday.

"This is a multi-year picture, and I think we've seen those changes both in the U.S. and also Europe," he said, referencing internal budgetary difficulties and questions around NATO's hiked spending targets.

Morningstar analyst Loredana Muharremi told CNBC Wednesday's weakness in European defense stocks was largely sentiment-driven.

"Some investors may now see a greater chance of stronger US backing for Europe, easing the pressure on European governments to spend more [but] even with stronger US backing, we expect Europe to stick to its spending plans," she said.

"It takes years of investment to close the gaps in Europe's own military capabilities and build the industrial capacity it needs to deter Russia."

Lazio, la metamorfosi di Isaksen (pronto al rinnovo): 4 gol in 5 gare per il danese

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Vola la Lazio. La squadra di Gennaro Gattuso, almeno per questa settimana ed in attesa del match del weekend contro il Napoli al "Maradona", si gode il primato in classifica solitario. Un momento d'oro per i biancocelesti che hanno approcciato questa stagione come meglio non avrebbero potuto. Nove vittorie ed un pareggio in dieci giornate, quello casalingo contro il Milan partendo dal 2-0 di vantaggio. Ma soprattutto possono godere di un elemento decisivo in più per il reparto offensivo.

Gustav Isaksen è il simbolo di questo momento dei capitolini. Dalla sosta il tecnico lo ha schierato sempre titolare e lui l'ha ripagato con prestazioni e con gol decisivi. Solo una volta dal primo minuto nelle prime cinque gare e zero gol. Sempre nell'undici nelle seconde cinque e un bottino di quattro reti e un assist. La prima rete l’ha firmata nel successo per 3-1 sul Monza, poi la seconda sette giorni più tardi contro la Juventus, battuta 3-0 allo Stadium. Pope Leo sets off on 12-day South America tour

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The pontiff will hold meetings and celebrate Mass in Uruguay, Argentina and Peru, with the latter attracting particular interest. The trip will be the longest and farthest of his papacy so far.

The plane carrying Pope Leo XIV to South America for the beginning of his almost two-week tour set off from Rome on Friday.

Following a 13-and-a-half-hour flight, the pontiff is set to land in Uruguay, where he will spend three days before heading on to Argentina, and finally Peru.

The 71-year-old US-born pope is recovering from a lung cancer surgery that took place just three weeks ago.

Latin America is home to around 40% of the world's 1.4 billion Catholics, for whom the pope is their spiritual leader. However, fewer and fewer people on the continent identify as Catholic.

Pope Leo's first leg of the tour takes him to Uruguay, the most secular Latin American country.

It was the first country in the world to legalize recreational marijuana use and has since legalized abortion, same-sex marriage and assisted dying, all against opposition from the Catholic Church.

However, the enthusiasm around Leo's visit has come as a bit of a surprise, with tickets for his prayer vigil at Montevideo's Centenario Stadium selling out in just two days.

The trip will begin with a meeting with President Yamandu Orsi, leader of a center-left alliance. He is also planning to visit the Casavalle neighborhood, which is notorious for its high rates of poverty and violence.

With the first papal visit to Uruguay since 1988, Leo will be hoping to reinvigorate the faith in a country where only a fraction of believers attend Mass.

On Sunday, Leo will cross over to neighboring Argentina, the home of his papal predecessor Francis, who, however, disappointed his native compatriots by never returning home as pope.

At the beginning of Francis's papacy, some 71% of Argentinians identified as Catholic, according to the Pew Research Center. By 2024, this had fallen to 58%.

Leo is set to meet with Argentinian President Javier Milei, who has come under criticism from the Catholic Church for his sweeping cuts that have hampered programs for the poor and needy.

He is also planning to meet with business leaders, trade unions, workers and social representatives.

The final leg of the trip, on Wednesday, will see Leo return to Peru, where he lived and preached for two decades.

There is excitement about his visit to Peru, with the pope planning to celebrate Mass in his old diocese of Chiclayo as well as in the mountain village of Santa Cruz de Succhabamba.

The pope will also celebrate Mass in the former Incan capital city of Cusco before honoring Peru's indigenous population by traveling to Pucallpa in the Peruvian Amazon.

The excitement can be seen in the street vendors selling T-shirts and rosaries with his likeness, while bakeries have been selling Leo bread.

Peru has also seen a weakening of the faithful in recent years, with the Catholic population falling from 76% in 2013-2014 to around 67% now.

Jamie Carragher insists he’s glad Unai Emery is not moving to Manchester United

Ruairidh is a leading expert in La Liga and Spanish football. He's the editor of the #1 Spanish football publication... read more

Stan Collymore is a football pundit and former player, signing for Liverpool FC in 1995 for an English transfer record... read more

Aston Villa manager Unai Emery looks set to stay and sign a new contract at the club, and Jamie Carragher insists he’s glad he won’t be taking the Manchester United job.

Emery had been linked with the Red Devils, and Carragher attracted controversy for his comments insisting that the Spanish tactician would undoubtedly be leaving for a bigger club.

Villa were furious with Carragher’s comments and threatened legal action before he apologised, though he’s now written about it again in his latest column for the Telegraph.

Want more CaughtOffside coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

The pundit insists there’s no denying that Man Utd are bigger than Villa, even if the latter is arguably the better place to be at the moment.

Either way, he’s just relieved Emery looks set to stay with AVFC as he would only have made United better, which, as a former Liverpool player, is obviously not something he’d want to see.

“My argument has never been that Emery should leave to join a bigger club. Why would I want Emery to join Manchester United? It would make them stronger,” Carragher said.

“When he commits to a new deal it is great for Villa and the Premier League, underlining how settled he is.

“But it is a valid question whether being consistently praised as a coach who thrives at teams who are not expected to challenge for league titles and the Champions League has become a patronising, backhanded compliment.

“No world-class manager – and Emery is certainly that – can be happy being perceived as “better outside the elite”. That reputation is based on a difficult period at Arsenal, and a spell at Paris St-Germain where he enjoyed domestic success. He fell short in Europe in Paris, so is bound to be compared unfavourably with Luis Enrique.”

He added: “United clearly are not at the level of Real Madrid, Bayern Munich and PSG right now. But when any manager goes to Old Trafford, over the course of their contract they will be expected to build a team that will go head to head with those European giants. Anything less is failure.

Villa’s threat of legal action certainly seems a bit silly, with Carragher explaining himself quite well here, even if he perhaps went a little overboard with the tone of his comments before.

Darian Mensah's NFL Draft projection: Why Heisman front-runner isn't higher on 2027 draft boards

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Darian Mensah's NFL Draft projection: Why Heisman front-runner isn't higher on 2027 draft boards originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The 2026 college football season began with expectations that it might preview one of the strongest NFL quarterback classes in recent memory. Instead, the perceived top prospects have been all over the map.

Arch Manning and Dante Moore have slid, while Drew Mestemaker has surged and CJ Carr has held steady. No one might be having a better season than Miami's Darian Mensah, though.

Mensah has 25 touchdowns to just one interception through eight games, completing more than 80 percent of passes and positioning himself as a Heisman Trophy front-runner.

Still, not all NFL front offices are sold on Mensah's status as a high first-round pick.

Here's a closer look at Mensah's NFL Draft stock and why he isn't ranked higher.

MORE:The 10 best true freshmen in college football

Pundits are divided on Mensah's NFL Draft outlook. ESPN's Mel Kiper Jr. ranks Mensah fourth among all prospects in 2027, while Sporting News' own Vinnie Iyer doesn't have Mensah in the first round of his most recent NFL mock draft.

CBS Sports, meanwhile, ranks Mensah 81st among all prospects, signaling how many different opinions exist about the Miami quarterback.

If half of NFL teams view him as a legitimate first-round prospect and half don't, odds are Mensah is going to land on the higher end of that spectrum. All it takes is one team to believe in a quarterback for that quarterback to come off the board early, and Mensah's production has been so dominant that it wouldn't be surprising to see him land near the top of the first round.

Here are the details behind Mensah's inconsistent draft stock.

As a player, Mensah's arm strength is a potential red flag for NFL teams. His arm strength isn't a true weakness, but it doesn't jump off the page like it does for many premier NFL quarterback prospects who are jumping into a league that plays faster all-around.

Mensah's ball placement has easily made up for solid but unspectacular arm strength at the college level, but NFL teams will have to evaluate whether they believe that will stand up at the next level.

The inconsistent evaluations of Mensah might also be related to Miami's schedule this season. The Hurricanes simply haven't faced many high-level defenses, making it tougher to evaluate his decision-making. That is set to change, of course, as Miami will face Notre Dame and should be in line for at least one big-time matchup in the College Football Playoff.

If Mensah can come close to sustaining his production against tougher competition, he might win over some skeptical evaluators.

XRP sellers face massive slip as USD order book depth fakes size

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At the Nov. 4 close, a capped sample of Bitstamp-issued USD offers held less funded inventory than the smaller RLUSD book.

An XRP Ledger order book sample advertised 1.28 million Bitstamp-issued USD tokens at the Nov. 4 close, but the offer owners' balances funded only about 57,000.

For an XRP holder selling into those offers, the headline quantity overstated the token inventory available to trade.

The smaller sample for dollar stablecoin Ripple USD (RLUSD) held about 92,000 funded tokens, with no gap between quoted and funded amounts at that ledger.

A Nov. 5 refresh, at ledger 108132129, closed at 13:09 UTC, still showed a large Bitstamp-dollar gap of roughly 1.31 million tokens quoted against 58,179 funded. RLUSD had about 80,435 quoted and 75,726 funded, so its earlier fully funded reading had not persisted.

The Nov. 4 reading covered validated ledger 108120236, closed at 23:59:51 UTC. Bitstamp-issued USD offers totaled 1,280,737 tokens quoted and 56,887 funded, a funded share of 4.44%.

One offer advertised 1,241,760 USD tokens while its funded amount was only 17,967. That single offer accounted for almost the entire difference, showing why adding advertised order sizes can mislead about what a counterparty can deliver.

The measurement requests at most 100 offers per side and counts quantities within 10% of each book's own reference midpoint, derived from its tightest uncrossed bid and ask. NOSHASHI, the ledger-data service, calls the dollar-token side that XRP sellers receive the “ask.”

XRPL's offer rules allow an owner to advertise more than it currently holds because placing an offer does not lock its full amount. The official book offers interface normally omits unfunded offers and identifies partially funded amounts, so software can distinguish advertised size from supported size.

An oversized Bitstamp USD offer drove the November 4 gap. Compare both book samples at the next day's measured ledger.

A larger advertised book can contain less funded token inventory for XRP sellers.

Book bars share a linear 0 to 1.4 million token-unit scale; funded shares use unrounded inputs and two decimals. Bitstamp's IOU explanation describes redemption through the issuing platform and an account there. A holder's access to US dollars' redemption still depends on the applicable account and issuer conditions.

RLUSD's user terms name Standard Custody & Trust Company LLC as issuer, so direct redemption with Ripple requires being an eligible Customer under a separate agreement. Receiving either token on-ledger differs from receiving spendable dollars in a bank account.

Using the Nov. 5 ledger, a static model swept funded offers in price order for a 10,000-XRP sale. A separate model used each automated market maker (AMM), which trades against pool reserves, applying its ordinary trading fee in a constant-product calculation.

The book estimates use the 100-offer cap and 10% band. Pool estimates include ordinary AMM trading fees, and all four exclude additional issuer-transfer, network, and redemption costs, as well as wallet and trust-line constraints.

In the same model, a 50,000-XRP sale exhausted the sampled Bitstamp-dollar offers after about 47,484 XRP, returning 58,179 USD tokens and leaving 2,516 XRP unsold. The sampled RLUSD offers absorbed the modeled 50,000 XRP for about 64,960 RLUSD.

The RLUSD AMM held about 1.83 million XRP and 2.39 million RLUSD, versus only about 3,489 XRP and 4,594 USD tokens in the Bitstamp-dollar pool.

Quoted size, funded inventory, and completed trading volume answer different questions. For a seller, the useful test is a refreshed estimate for the intended size and acceptable issuer token, including the costs and redemption access needed to turn that token into cash.

Hutchinson a CBS: "Tanti di quelli che non giocano non hanno mostrato la fame di volere una maglia da titolare"

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Omari Hutchinson, attaccante del Milan, è stato intervistato da CBS Sports Golazo al termine di Milan-Ferencvaros (0-2) di Europa League.

"Penso che abbiamo avuto un inizio molto lento. Credo che mentalmente fossimo preparati: ci avevano mostrato tutte le tattiche, cosa avremmo dovuto fare e come iniziare. Credo che ultimamente stessimo avendo parecchi avvii a rilento, e anche se nel secondo tempo siamo scesi in campo con un atteggiamento un po' più positivo, oggi semplicemente non è bastato. Non credo che abbiamo dimostrato quella fame di arrivare sulla palla, di incidere sotto porta. Quindi sì, è stato tutto molto, molto lento".

E cosa bisogna fare nelle prossime partite per la vostra prestazione e per il vostro modo di giocare?

"Penso solo che dobbiamo giocare come abbiamo fatto contro l'Inter. Potevi sentire l'energia nello spogliatoio, si percepiva che volevamo vincere. Penso che oggi non abbiamo mostrato molta umiltà. Non abbiamo voluto... insomma, tanti giocatori che non giocano non hanno mostrato quella fame di volere una maglia da titolare, e penso che questo non sia stato sufficiente. Credo che domenica dovremo partire forte ed essere davvero bravi con la palla".

Riuscirete a trovare la forza per risalire la classifica?

"Sì, sì, penso che i giochi non siano chiusi finché non siamo fuori, quindi dobbiamo solo continuare a concentrarci. Sì, dobbiamo solo migliorare. Abbiamo un buon gruppo, lo staff ci aiuta sempre, ci mostra quello che dobbiamo fare. E ora credo che tocchi a noi. Penso che oggi abbiamo deluso tutti e abbiamo un'altra partita domenica, dobbiamo concentrarci su quella e scendere in campo con forza".

Flaminio, Lazio e Soprintendenza verso l'intesa sulla pensilina

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Per la capienza si ragiona su 47-48mila posti, meno dei 50.570 previsti dalla proposta iniziale del club.

Il progetto della Lazio per lo stadio Flaminio procede, anche se lentamente: sarebbe stata trovata un’intesa con la Soprintendenza per conservare la pensilina originaria. Sul fronte della capienza, le parti sembrano essersi avvicinate a una soluzione tra 47mila e 48mila posti, mentre il club proverà a raggiungere i 50.570 posti previsti nella proposta iniziale. Il confronto va avanti attraverso compromessi, ma restano resistenze da superare.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il numero di spettatori è uno dei temi principali del confronto tra la Lazio e la Soprintendenza. Da giorni sembra essere stata raggiunta un’intesa su una capienza compresa tra 47mila e 48mila posti: una cifra inferiore ai 50.570 prospettati inizialmente dal club biancoceleste. La Lazio continuerà a puntare a quell’obiettivo, ma non è scontato che riesca a ottenerlo.

Per arrivare alla capienza immaginata nella proposta di partenza dovranno essere superate le resistenze ancora presenti. L’accordo che sembra delinearsi sui 47mila-48mila posti rappresenta dunque un avvicinamento tra le parti, senza garantire che il numero possa salire fino a quello richiesto dalla Lazio. Il dialogo prosegue insieme agli altri passaggi necessari per far avanzare il progetto.

Un altro passo avanti riguarderebbe la pensilina originaria: la soluzione sulla quale sarebbe stato trovato un accordo ne prevede la conservazione. È uno degli elementi originali più riconoscibili e peculiari del progetto di Nervi, pensato per le Olimpiadi del 1960. Preservarla era un nodo cruciale per proseguire l’iter, soprattutto per il rapporto con l’inserimento del secondo anello e della nuova copertura.

La soluzione condivisa permetterebbe di mantenere il progetto originale e, allo stesso tempo, di far convivere la vecchia struttura con quella moderna. La tutela della pensilina si affianca così al confronto sulla capienza: sono i due temi sui quali il dialogo tra Lazio e Soprintendenza sembra aver prodotto passi avanti, pur senza chiudere tutte le questioni legate al progetto.

Sono 900 i biglietti acquistati dai tifosi della Lazio per la trasferta di domenica al Maradona. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Corriere dello Sport, Il settore ospiti comincia a riempirsi nonostante le limitazioni alla vendita. Restano poco meno di 1.600 posti disponibili, ma arrivare a un altro tutto esaurito sarà complicato.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il dato dei 900 tagliandi venduti lascia ancora una parte del settore ospiti libera. La possibilità di acquistare un biglietto, però, è ridotta dalle restrizioni previste per la partita, come riferisce Il Corriere dello Sport.

La vendita è vietata a tutti i residenti nella Regione Lazio, compresi quelli in possesso della tessera del tifoso. Il provvedimento restringe sensibilmente la platea dei potenziali acquirenti.

Le prime trasferte avevano visto un seguito continuo, fino agli oltre 10.000 laziali presenti a Reggio Emilia. Al Maradona il numero dei sostenitori sarà inevitabilmente più basso, ma la squadra non sarà completamente sola.

Steele dominates Clemens to cap unblemished District 27-6A championship

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Led by its stingy defense, Steele wrapped up a dominant run through District 27-6A with a 42-14 victory over Clemens on Thursday at Lehnhoff Stadium.

Steele (8-1-1, 7-0) racked up 508 yards of total offense, while holding Clemens (7-3, 4-3) to 184 total yards, as the Knights defeated their rivals for the seventh straight time.

The Knights came into the game having already clinched their seventh straight district title, but Steele didn't rest on its laurels as it outscored Clemens 35-14 after the game was tied 7-7 early in the second quarter.

Steele coach David Saenz said winning the district title for the seventh year in a row was a big accomplishment for the senior class, a group which hasn't lost a district game in four years.

"We always talk about protecting the tradition," Saenz said. "It feels good and extremely proud, especially for our seniors to go the four years that they were here without losing a district game and then continuing that tradition of winning a district championship."

Steele senior Marshall Kenner rushes into the end zone during a game between Steele and Clemens at Lehnhoff Stadium on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026, in Schertz. (Andrew J. Whitaker/San Antonio Express-News)

With the win, Steele leads the all-time series against Clemens 16-2, with the Buffaloes last win against the Knights in 2019.

Next up, Steele hosts Reagan (6-3, 6-1) in the bi-district round of the Class 6A Division I playoffs next Thursday at Lehnhoff Stadium. Kickoff is set for 7:30 p.m.

Clemens needed a win to clinch a playoff berth. Now the Buffaloes' playoff hopes hinge on the longshot chance that winless San Marcos (0-9, 0-6) can beat East Central (5-4, 3-3) Friday in the regular season finale at 7:30 p.m. at Hornet Stadium. An East Central loss would secure Clemens' first playoff berth in seven years.

A Hornets win would result in a tiebreaker, but with Pieper and Judson now both a half-game up on Clemens going into Friday, it could be up to a four-team tie for the final three spots, depending on how Pieper and Judson fare in their matchups against Wagner and New Braunfels Canyon, respectively.

The Hornets hold the head-to-head tiebreaker over the Buffaloes if it comes down to a two-way tie.

Clemens coach Alan Hill said his team accomplished a lot in his third season as the Buffaloes' coach, as Clemens produced its first winning season in seven years.

"It was really good," Hill said. "Obviously refreshing around here for the kids to have a winning mindset and a winning mentality. I wish we would have put forth a little bit better showing tonight, but tough way to end the season against a really good opponent. Obviously can't take away from what the kids have done up to this point. Really proud of them."

Saenz said the Steele defense was able to dominate Clemens because of their physicality.

"They played physical, they were playing fast," Saenz said. "We knew they were going to come out, they had to win to get into the playoffs. Our guys, we're just proud of the way they adjusted to the different schemes they (Clemens) were trying to hit us on early and our defensive staff did a great job of recognizing them and putting our kids in good situations. The kids went out and they performed well."

Steele senior Jamin Harvell celebrates a touchdown during a game between Steele and Clemens at Lehnhoff Stadium on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026, in Schertz. (Andrew J. Whitaker/San Antonio Express-News)

Marshall Kenner, who rushed for 178 yards on 17 carries, put Steele up 7-0 when he scored on an 11-yard run at the 5:35 mark of the first quarter, capping off a nine-play, 78-yard drive.

Clemens came back on the ensuing possession, starting off with Zarion Jordan's 35-yard kickoff return. The Buffaloes went 60 yards in 10 plays, ending with Daniel Gray's 1-yard touchdown run on the first play of the second quarter to tie the game at 7-7.

After Gray's scoring run, Steele put the clamps on Clemens, as the Knights held the Buffaloes to 80 yards and only four first downs in the first half. Steele then ramped up its offense, as Kenner dashed for 63 yards on the next possession to the Clemens 5, setting up his own 5-yard touchdown run which put Knights up for good at 14-7.

Steele scored two more times in the second quarter on its way to a 28-7 halftime lead, with DJ Green throwing touchdown passes of 34 yards to Jamin Harvell and 76 yards to Jayden Casiano.

The Knights scored twice in the third quarter, on a 20-yard interception return by Teylen White and a 71-yard touchdown run by Quinton Humphrey-Davis to extend their lead to 42-7.

Late in the third quarter, Clemens put together a big play when backup quarterback Chase Stevens connected with Luis Santiago on a 42-yard pass to the Steele 21. The big play led to a 5-yard scoring run from Devin Morgan on the first play of the fourth quarter.

Green finished 8-of-11 passing for 185 yards and two scores with one interception for Steele.

The Knights' playoff opener will be a rematch from earlier this season as they thumped the Rattlers 52-21 in the season opener.

Saenz said Reagan is a much better team than the one his squad played to open the season.

"They've improved tremendously," Saenz said. "They've been playing extremely good football these last couple of weeks. So, we're going to have to make sure we prepare and continue to get better at all three phases at our end too."

Leicester City confirm departure of Barnsley FC icon Conor Hourihane as ex-Leeds United man assumes new role

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Barnsley icon Conor Hourihane has left Leicester City less than five months on from his appointment as assistant manager.

The 35-year-old was a Reds hero in his playing days and made 136 appearances in the club’s colours. He later took the reins as the club’s head coach, but left at the end of last season.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

He bounced back into coaching with Leicester, taking up a role alongside Russell Martin.

However, just months into his spell as Martin’s number two, Hourihane has left the third-tier Foxes. Martin will now have coaching support from ex-Leeds United forward Steve Morison, who will combine coaching duties with his responsibilities as loans manager.

A club spokesperson said: “We can confirm that Leicester City assistant manager Conor Hourihane has left the football club. We thank Conor for his contribution during his time with us and wish him well for the future.

“First-team manager Russell Martin will continue to be supported by the existing coaching staff of Andy King, Tom Weal and Rhys Owen.

“Steve Morison will also provide coaching support to the first team alongside his existing responsibilities as loans manager.”

Hourihane has taken to Instagram to address his Leicester exit, insisting he is a “better coach” for his time with the club.

He said: “Wishing everyone at Leicester City all the very best moving forward, it’s a brilliant club with brilliant people. Really enjoyed my time and learned so much to make me a better coach going into my next role.”

Fans were left to wonder why Hourihane had left so early into his Foxes stay, but head coach Russell Martin has quelled speculation with a simple explanation.

He said: “I think purely down to a really honest conversation between me and Conor. He really wants to be a manager again. I'm really grateful he joined as a coach and wanted to learn.

“There's been opportunities that have come up here and abroad that maybe would have interested him if he'd been out of work. So we have to recognise that we want people here that are all in. It frees him up to take an opportunity.”

Retired midfielder Hourihane, who also had a loan spell at Sheffield United during his playing career, initially joined Barnsley in 2014. He was an influential figure during an exciting chapter in the club’s history, helping the Reds climb from League One into the Championship.

He left in 2017 before returning seven years later, taking up a player-coach role during the reign of Darrell Clarke. Hourihane hung up his boots shortly into his second Oakwell stay, focusing solely on his coaching duties.

He was handed the main role on a full-time basis when Clarke was dispensed of, but could not push the Reds into the League One promotion picture.

lavazza tablì wins designboom special prize third place at seoul design award

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

lavazza's tablì tabs eliminates capsules from the single-cup experience.

Sustainability flourishes in simplicity. Caring for the planet becomes easy when real, convenient and functional alternatives replace more polluting options. Lavazza kept this reality in mind with Tablì, a capsule-free single-serve coffee system that has earned third place in the designboom Special Prize at the Seoul Design Award 2026.

Until now, coffee drinkers have had three main options: go through the hassle of preparing a pot each time they wanted to enjoy the drink, use instant coffee or turn to a capsule machine. The third option is widespread because of its convenience, but there is a hidden side that not everyone is aware of.

According to figures from Deutsche Umwelthilfe, cited by Lavazza in its award submission, around 20 billion coffee capsules are used, and therefore discarded, every year. Germany alone consumes around 3.4 billion, generating approximately 13,500 metric tons of waste annually.

Hailing from Italy, a country where coffee is both a rule and a tradition, the multidisciplinary team, led by Florian Seidl and joined by Angelica Rella, Marco Rotundo and Nicola Bettoni, was well aware of this problem.

The team posed a revolutionary question: what if there were another way to prepare coffee? Tablì was born from the ambition to replicate the convenience of capsule coffee while designing a product with minimal environmental impact.

Their vision went beyond replacing capsules with a less harmful form of packaging. Instead, they designed a system built around a tab made entirely of coffee. No plastic, aluminum, additives or coatings. Alongside the tabs, they completed the ecosystem by designing a compatible machine, packaging, accessories and a connectivity system.

Lavazza Tablì considers environmental care from production through maintenance and everyday use. The machine is made using post-consumer plastics, and its construction takes repairability and low energy consumption into account. Moreover, because the tabs are made entirely of coffee, they generate only organic waste.

This commitment to sustainability has been key to securing the Special Prize with designboom. The Seoul Design Award’s purpose is precisely to promote projects that have achieved harmony between people, society and the environment.

The designboom Special Prize introduces a series of additional requirements. Award-winning projects must connect with designboom’s values: Intentionally Curious, Disrupt Playfully, and Fearlessly Meaningful. Ultimately, they must demonstrate an ability to think outside the box, create products that bring us joy and pursue design that, beyond being beautiful, expresses a bold vision with functionality rooted in its very conception.

Lavazza Tablì has the honor of taking third place in the category for its attention to detail, aesthetics and functionality as a whole rather than as separate elements. The product’s specifications speak for themselves: the height-adjustable cup rest with a metal grid; the sliding opening for inserting the tab; and the transparent water tank that allows users to check the water level are examples of a project conceived for intuitive, smooth operation that elevates the user experience while protecting the environment.

Discover more about the designboom Special Prize and explore the full gallery of Lavazza Tablì — here.

‘I hate the man’: Recalling Alex Ferguson’s first fight at Man Utd with their City-supporting bus driver

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sir Alex Ferguson took only two days to pick his first fight as Manchester United manager – with their over-familiar, City-supporting bus driver.

Ferguson was appointed 40 years ago when he moved to Old Trafford from Aberdeen to take over from the sacked Ron Atkinson and join “the biggest club in football”.

When he moved south on November 6, 1986, the Scot encountered a sleeping giant, positioned then in 20th place in Division One, and immediately identified a culture that needed to change if United were ever to reach the top again.

“Look, I’ll tell you right now, I won’t change,” Ferguson told the squad upon gathering them in the gymnasium at The Cliff training ground on his first day.

“I won’t change my beliefs in what a football club should be about… I’m not going to change. You’ll have to change.”

Ferguson’s first target was unexpectedly identified during his first match on his third of 9,631 days in charge at Old Trafford.

Derek Sutton had driven Ferguson and the United squad to Oxford United for the new manager’s first game on Saturday November 8.

Fergie was first off the Finglands coach and swallowed up by a media scrum on his way into the Manor Ground.

With his cargo unloaded, Sutton then did what he always did: parked his bus, then parked his butt on the United bench.

Having the bus driver in the dug-out was as standard as a Sunday piss-up for United then. Even if Sutton was a Manchester City fan.

Just a fortnight earlier, in Atkinson’s penultimate match as United boss, Sutton was in the City dug-out at Maine Road after driving United to the derby.

“The thing that always makes me laugh when I remember these games is that our coach driver Derek – or Del Boy as we used to call him – would drive us to Maine Road and then go and sit on the City bench,” Atkinson recalled for the BBC.

“He was a funny man and a great laugh. He normally sat in the dug-out with me and he used to make the teas for us at half-time but he was a City fan, and was their driver too.”

Ferguson did not know that ‘Del Boy’ was a City fan. He barely knew him at all. But Ferguson quickly decided his bus driver was overstepping when the new manager left his seat in the main stand with United 1-0 down towards the end of his first half in charge.

“I went down just before half time and in the dug-out, the bus driver was sitting there,” Ferguson later recalled to ITV.

“I didn’t say anything, I just thought ‘this is interesting’.

“So we went in at half-time and the bus driver is in the dressing room pouring the tea for the players.

“I said ‘excuse me, Derek. I don’t need you. Don’t come in the dressing room, don’t come in the dug-out again’.”

United physio Jim McGregor – far left on the bench in the photo above, with substitute Jesper Olsen, reserve-team manager Brian Whitehouse and kit-man Norman Davies between him and Ferguson, while Sutton sits next to the manager for the first and last time – and goalkeeper Chris Turner have backed up Fergie’s version of events.

But Sutton later disputed it. And, like most time-served City fans, he holds a sizeable grudge towards the United legend.

“Ferguson never told me to my face that he didn’t want me in the dressing room, he did that behind my back to my bosses at the coach firm,” Sutton said in 2004.

“I hate the man – he is a very hateful person.

“I was always in the dug-out until Ferguson came along. I always served the tea at half-time.

“Atkinson brought me in and he was always sound with me. I’d have passes to walk anywhere around Old Trafford and sat on the bench for the 1985 FA Cup final at Wembley when United beat Everton.

“When Ferguson came in, he wanted to stamp out all the old regime.”

Ocarina Of Time Remake Devs Didn't Focus On The Zelda Timeline

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

For as long as The Legend of Zelda has existed, its timeline has caused confusion. Online debates have often raged over which games fall where on the timeline, and which timelines they even exist in to begin with.

With such a young development team, you'd expect The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Switch 2 remake to face a lot of timeline-related questions, especially since some developers admitted they hadn't played the original game. Zelda series producer Eiji Aonuma, who oversaw the remake, sought to simplify things with one piece of advice: just "focus on the game."

Spoilers for The Legend of Zelda timelines ahead.

The Legend of Zelda is split into multiple timelines. The Creation and Early Eras explores how Hyrule came to be and includes games like Skyward Sword and Ocarina of Time. Ocarina of Time then serves to split the series into three further timelines:

Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom then unite these timelines, telling the story of a distant legend. It's all a little bit messy, and with a fledgling development team, Aonuma revealed to Game Informer that he received lots of questions about it during development.

"It's funny because the staff would sometimes ask me about the possibility of including nods to the timeline or references to the other game," he said. "And I actually, at the time, would say, 'No, let's focus on this game. Let's make this the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can.' So, the timeline was in the back of our mind, but we didn't consider taking an approach to this game where we actually, explicitly included references like that."

No, let's focus on this game. Let's make this the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can.

Naturally, when you're developing a Zelda game, the timeline has to be considered, but as a remake, this makes total sense. The new Ocarina of Time team was redoing a self-contained story, and one where the timeline had already been determined. They didn't need to focus on the cause and effect, just making the best version of the game possible, and they seemed to do just that.

It's now comfortably the best-reviewed game of 2026, at least until we find out what GTA 6 scores, and it received a 5/5 from us here at TheGamer. In our review, we said, "Nintendo’s remake feels like a triumphant celebration of what made it so special while wisely modernizing small aspects to make for a more enjoyable experience," praising the new art style, auditory updates, and gameplay balance.
